Topological equivariant coarse 𝐊 -homology \mathclap{\text{Topological equivariant coarse $\bm{K}$-homology}}
Ulrich Bunke, Alexander Engel

Abstract
For a C ∗ C^{*} -category with a strict G G -action we construct examples of equivariant coarse homology theories. To this end we first introduce versions of Roe categories of objects in C ∗ C^{*} -categories which are controlled over bornological coarse spaces, and then apply a homological functor. These equivariant coarse homology theories are then employed to verify that certain functors on the orbit category are CP-functors. This fact has consequences for the injectivity of assembly maps.



1
Introduction

Let
G
be a group.
In this paper we construct and study several versions of
G
-equivariant coarse homology theories listed in (
1.1
) below which are equivariant analogs of the coarse topological
K
-homology
K\!{\mathcal{X}}
constructed in
[
BE20b
]
. They depend on the choices of a coefficient
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbf{C}}
with
G
-action and a homological functor
\mathrm{Hg}\colon C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathbf{M}
. The main example of a homological functor is the
K
-theory functor for possibly non-unital
C^{*}
-categories
\mathrm{K^{C^{*}Cat}}\colon C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathbf{Sp}
.

The present paper focusses on those details of the construction of the homology theories which are related to coarse geometry.
For the results about
C^{*}
-categories going into the construction and the notion of a homological functor we refer to the companion papers
[
BEa
]
,
[
Bun
]
which were partly written to provide the background for the present paper.
In addition, the main definitions and facts will be recalled in Section
3
.

One of the main applications of the coarse homology theory
\mathrm{K^{C^{*}Cat}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c}
introduced in the present paper is the verification of the CP-condition
[
BEKW20d
, Def. 1.8]
for the functors on the orbit category of
G

defined in
[
BEa
, Def. 19.12]
. As shown in
[
BEKW20d
]

this CP-condition implies split-injectivity results for assembly maps. This application will be discussed in some detail in this introduction further below.
For further applications to index theory we refer to
[
BEb
,
BL
]
.
The results of the present paper are the crucial technical input for the discussion of equivariant Paschke duality in
[
BELa
]
.

In the present paper we work in the setup of equivariant coarse homotopy theory as developed in
[
BEKW20b
]
which generalizes the non-equivariant case from
[
BE20b
]
. Its basic category is the category

G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
of
G
-bornological coarse spaces introduced in Definition
2.10
.
By Definition
2.37
an equivariant coarse homology theory is a functor

E\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to\mathbf{M}

with a stable target

\infty
-category
\mathbf{M}
(e.g. the category
\mathbf{Sp}
of spectra),
which sends coarse equivalences to equivalences and annililates flasque spaces. It must furthermore satisfy
an excisiveness condition and the condition of
u
-continuity.
In Section
2
we give a complete and selfcontained introduction to the notions from coarse geometry and homotopy theory of bornological coarse spaces which are relevant for the present paper.
For different setups and axioms for coarse homology theories see
[
Mit01
,
Hei
,
Wul22
]
.

The output of our construction are four functors

\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G},\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c}^{G},\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G},\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c,G}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to\mathbf{M}

(1.1)

which are all equivariant coarse homology theories, see Sections
7
and
8
.
Our construction is basically a generalization of the construction of the non-equivariant coarse
K
-homology
K\!\mathcal{X}\colon\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to\mathbf{Sp}
in
[
BE20b
]
to the equivariant case and for general coefficients. Thereby we follow the structure of
the construction of coarse homology theories via controlled objects in left exact
\infty
-categories in
[
BCKW
]
.

One of the main achievements of
[
BE20b
]
is the construction of a refinement of Roe’s coarse
K
-homology to a spectrum-valued coarse homology theory defined the category
\mathbf{BornCoarse}
of bornological coarse spaces.
In
[
BE20b
,
BE20a
]
we observed that an interpretion of the coarse assembly map as a natural transformation between such spectrum-valued coarse homology theories allows for a considerable simplification of the formal structure of the proof of the coarse Baum-Connes conjecture given in
[
Wri05
]
.
The equivariant version of the coarse assembly map (also known as forget-control map) turned out to be a tool to study a different sort of assembly maps. Given a functor

M\colon G\mathbf{Orb}\to\mathbf{M}

with a cocomplete target
\mathbf{M}
and two families of subgroups
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
of
G
such that
{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
we have the assembly map

\mathrm{Asmbl}_{{\mathcal{F}},M}^{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}\colon\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{G_{{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbf{Orb}}M\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{G_{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}\mathbf{Orb}}M\,,

(1.2)

where
G_{{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbf{Orb}
is the full subcategory of
G\mathbf{Orb}
of orbits with stabilizers in
{\mathcal{F}}
.
One of the driving force for developing an equivariant coarse homotopy theory was the study of injectivity and isomorphism results for assembly maps (
1.2
) for various choices of the functor
M
and families of subgroups.
Initially this development was a part of
controlled topology

[
CP95
]
, see also
[
BLR08
,
Bar16
,
RTY14
]
. The connection with
coarse geometry is explained in
[
HPR96
]
.

We say that
a coarse homology theory
E\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to\mathbf{M}
extends the functor
M
if for every
S
in
G\mathbf{Orb}
we have a natural equivalence

M(S)\simeq E(S_{min,max}\otimes G_{can,min})\,,

(1.3)

where

S_{min,max}
is the
G
-bornological coarse space given by the
G
-orbit
S
with the minimal coarse structure and the maximal bornology, and
G_{can,min}
is the group
G
with the canonical coarse structure and the minimal bornology.
Such an extension of
M
to a coarse homology theory is one of the basic
ingredients of the approach of
[
BLR08
]
(isomorphism result, see also
[
BKW21
]
) or
[
RTY14
]

(injectivity result). In
[
BEKW20d
, Def. 1.8]
we axiomatized the additional conditions on
E
needed to show that
\mathrm{Asmbl}_{\mathbf{Fin},M}^{\mathbf{All}}
is split-injective by the notion of a CP-functor.

Hence more examples of coarse homology theories
E
leading to CP-functors
M
via (
1.3
) lead to more examples of functors on the orbit category for which one can show split injectivity of the assembly map by the method of
[
BEKW20d
]
. Examples are coarse ordinary homology and coarse algebraic
K
-theory with coefficients in an additive category with strict
G
-action
[
BEKW20b
]
(the CP-conditions are verified in
[
BEKW20d
]
),
the equivariant coarse version of Waldhausen algebraic
K
-theory of spaces

[
BDC19
]
, and the equivariant coarse
K
-homology with coefficients in a left-exact
\infty
-category with
G
-action constructed in

[
BCKW
]
. The present paper contributes
\mathrm{K^{C^{*}Cat}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c}
as a further example. We refer to Theorem
13.3
for a precise statement.

For a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
with
G
-action
we can consider the
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbf{C}}\coloneqq\mathbf{Hilb}_{c}(A)
of Hilbert-
A
-modules and compact operators with the
G
-action described in Example
3.5
. We then consider the functor

\mathrm{K^{C^{*}Cat}}{\mathbf{C}}_{G,r}\colon G\mathbf{Orb}\to\mathbf{Sp}

introduced in Definition
13.1
. By Proposition
9.2
this functor is extended (in the sense explained above) by the
coarse homology theory
\mathrm{K^{C^{*}Cat}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c}
.
By Theorem
13.3
it is a CP-functor.
In particular the split injectivity results from

[
BEKW20d
]
(see also
[
BCKW
, Sec. 6.5]
for a quick review) apply to
\mathrm{K^{C^{*}Cat}}{\mathbf{C}}_{G,r}
.
But note the following remark which explains that the above can not extend the class of groups
G
for which split-injectivity results are known.

Remark 1.1
.

If
A
is unital and has the trivial
G
-action, then by

[
BEa
, Prop. 19.18]
the functor
\mathrm{K^{C^{*}Cat}}{\mathbf{C}}_{G,r}
can be identified with
the functor
K^{\mathrm{DL},G}_{A}\colon G\mathbf{Orb}\to\mathbf{Sp}
introduced by Davis–Lück
[
DL98
]
.
1
1

1

To be precise, in
[
DL98
]
only the case
A={\mathbb{C}}
is considered but the generalization to general unital
C^{*}
-algebras
A
is straightforward.
We can thus consider
\mathrm{K^{C^{*}Cat}}{\mathbf{C}}_{G,r}
as a
generalization of the Davis–Lück construction to
C^{*}
-algebras with non-trivial
G
-action. An independent construction of such a functor

K^{\mathrm{DL},G}_{A}
for
C^{*}
-algebras with
G
-action
A
has been given recently by Kranz
[
Kra20
]
, see also
[
BELa
]
.
Note also that for every
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbf{C}}
with
G
-action there exists a suitable
C^{*}
-algebra with
G
-action
A
and an equivalence

\mathrm{K^{C^{*}Cat}}{\mathbf{C}}_{G,r}\simeq K^{\mathrm{DL},G}_{A}
. Hence taking
C^{*}
-categories instead of
C^{*}
-algebras with
G
-action as coefficients does not provide additional equivalence classes of functors on the orbit category.

By passing to homotopy groups the assembly map
\mathrm{Asmbl}_{\mathbf{Fin},K^{\mathrm{DL},G}_{A}}^{\mathbf{All}}
becomes isomorphic to the usual Baum–Connes assembly map

\mu^{BC}_{A,*}\colon RK_{*}^{G}(E_{\mathbf{Fin}}G,A)\to K_{*}^{C^{*}}(A\rtimes_{r}G)

(1.4)

defined via Kasparov’s
K\!K
-theory,
where the domain denotes the equivariant, locally finite
K
-homology of
E_{\mathbf{Fin}}G
with coefficients in
A
. This is shown (independently and with completely different methods) in
[
Kra20
]
and in

[
BELa
]
.

In
[
STY02
]
it is shown that the Baum–Connes assembly map
\mu^{BC}_{A,*}
is split-injective provided the countable group
G
with its canonical coarse structure
G_{can}
admits a coarse embedding into a Hilbert space. In the following we explain why this result is more general than what could possibly be shown by specializing the results from
[
BEKW20d
]
to
M
. To this end we note that
the main assumption on
G
going into the split-injectivity results in
[
BEKW20d
]
is
finite decomposition complexity (FDC) for
G_{can}
.
In
[
GTY13
]
it is shown that this FDC condition implies that
G_{can}

admits a coarse embedding into a Hilbert space. Hence
all split-injectivity results obtained by specializing
[
BEKW20d
]
to equivariant topolological
K
-homology are already
covered by
[
STY02
]
.

But note that the method of the proof in
[
STY02
]
is specific to equivariant topological
K
-theory with coefficients in a
C^{*}
-algebra, while the results from
[
BEKW20d
]
apply to a variety of other functors on the orbit category which are not related with topological
K
-theory at all.

∎

We now describe the structure of the present paper in some detail.
The input data for our construction is a
C^{*}
-category with
G
-action
{\mathbf{C}}
. In Section
4

we associate to every
G
-set
X
a
C^{*}
-category
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(X)
of
X
-controlled
G
-objects in the multiplier category
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
of
{\mathbf{C}}
. If
X
has the structure of a
G
-bornological coarse space, then in Section
5
we use the additional structures on
X
in order to define smallness conditions on the objects of
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(X)
and control conditions on the morphisms. As a result we obtain
versions of Roe categories
\bar{\mathbf{C}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}_{?}(X)
. In Section

6
we show the basic properties
of the functors
X\mapsto\bar{\mathbf{C}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}_{?}(X)
which go into the verification of the axioms of a coarse homology theory later.
In Sections
7
and
8
we define our four versions of coarse homology theories listed in (
1.1
) and show that they have the required properties.
In Section
9
we calculate the values of these functors on certain
G
-bornological spaces. These calculations are relevant for identifying the corresponding functors
M
on
G\mathbf{Orb}
defined via (
1.3
).
The Sections
10
and
12
prepare the proof of Theorem
13.3
in Section
13
by showing that
\mathrm{K^{C^{*}Cat}}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c}
admit transfers (an additional structure) and is strongly additive (an additional property).
The Section
11
is of independent interest since it relates equivariant coarse homology theories for different groups via transfer and induction. The results of that section will be used in
[
BL
]
.

Acknowledgement: U.B. was supported by the SFB 1085 (Higher Invariants) funded by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG).

2
Bornological coarse spaces and equivariant coarse homology theories

In this section we recall the category of
G
-bornological coarse spaces
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
and the notion of an equivariant coarse homology
theory. The reference for the equivariant case is
[
BEKW20b
]
, but see also
[
BE20b
]
for the non-equivariant case.

In order to fix size issues we fix four Grothendieck universes. Their elements are called very small sets, small sets, large sets, and very large sets, respectively.

Let
G
be a very small group. We let
{\mathbf{Set}}
denote the small category of very small sets. We further let

G{\mathbf{Set}}\coloneqq{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,{\mathbf{Set}})

be the small category of very small sets with
G
-action and equivariant maps.

If
X
is a set, then
\mathcal{P}_{X}
denotes the power set of
X
. The elements of
\mathcal{P}_{X\times X}
can be composed or inverted in the sense of correspondences (multi-valued maps). For
B
in
\mathcal{P}_{X}
and
U
in
\mathcal{P}_{X\times X}
we let
U[B]
in
\mathcal{P}_{X}
be the result of applying the correspondence
U
to
B
.

If

X
is in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
, then
G
acts on
\mathcal{P}_{X}
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
G
-coarse structure on
X
is a subset
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
of
\mathcal{P}_{X\times X}
satisfying the following conditions:

1.

{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
is
G
-invariant.

2.

{\mathtt{diag}}(X)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
.

3.

{\mathcal{C}}
is closed under forming subsets, finite unions, inverses and compositions.

4.

The sub-poset (w.r.t. the inclusion relation) of
G
-invariants
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}^{G}
is cofinal in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
.

The elements of
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
are called coarse entourages.

A subset
A
of
\mathcal{P}_{X\times X}
generates a smallest
G
-coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}\langle A\rangle
on
X
containing
A
.

We consider two
G
-sets with
G
-coarse structures
(X,{\mathcal{C}}_{X})
and
(X^{\prime},{\mathcal{C}}_{X^{\prime}})
, and a morphism
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
.

Definition 2.2
.

The map
f
is controlled if
(f\times f)({\mathcal{C}}_{X})\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}_{X^{\prime}}
.

Definition 2.3
.

A
G
-coarse space is a pair
(X,{\mathcal{C}}_{X})
of a
G
-set
X
with a
G
-coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
. A morphism between
G
-coarse spaces is an equivariant controlled map.

We obtain the category
G\mathbf{Coarse}
of
G
-coarse spaces and equivariant, controlled maps.
Usually we will shorten the notation and denote
G
-coarse spaces just by the symbol
X
.

Example 2.4
.

Let
(X,d)
be a metric space equipped with an isometric
G
-action.
The metric
G
-coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}_{d}
on
X
is generated by the family of entourages
(U_{r})_{r\in[0,\infty)}
, where
U_{r}\coloneqq\{(x,x^{\prime})\in X\times X\>|\>d(x,x^{\prime})\leq r\}
.

∎

Remark 2.5
.

If one drops Condition
4
in Definition
2.1
, then one gets the characterization of a coarse space with a
G
-action, i.e., of an object of
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathbf{Coarse})
.

For
\lambda
in
{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
we can consider the group
{\mathbb{Z}}
acting by
(n,x)\mapsto\lambda^{n}x
on the coarse space
{\mathbb{R}}
with the coarse structure induced from the standard metric. This is an example of a coarse space with a
G
-action by automorphisms. But if
|\lambda|\not=1
, then it is not a
G
-coarse space.

In contrast, if we let
{\mathbb{Z}}
act on
{\mathbb{R}}
by translations
(n,x)\mapsto n+x
, then this action is isometric and we would get a
G
-coarse space.

∎

Let
X
be in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
.

Definition 2.6
.

A
G
-bornology on
X
is a subset
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
of
\mathcal{P}_{X}
satisfying the following conditions:

1.

{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
is
G
-invariant.

2.

{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
is closed under forming subsets and finite unions.

3.

\bigcup_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}_{X}}B=X
.

The elements of
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
will be called the bounded subsets of
X
.

A subset
A
of
\mathcal{P}_{X}
generates a smallest
G
-bornology
{\mathcal{B}}\langle A\rangle
containing
A
.

We consider two
G
-sets with
G
-bornologies
(X,{\mathcal{B}}_{X})
and
(X^{\prime},{\mathcal{B}}_{X^{\prime}})
, and a map
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
.

Definition 2.7
.

1.

f
is proper if
f^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}_{X^{\prime}})\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
.

2.

f
is bornological if
f({\mathcal{B}}_{X})\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{X^{\prime}}
.

Definition 2.8
.

A
G
-bornological space is a pair
(X,{\mathcal{B}}_{X})
of a
G
-set
X
and a
G
-bornological structure
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
.
A morphism between
G
-bornological spaces is an equivariant and proper map.

We obtain the category
G\mathbf{Born}
of
G
-bornological spaces and equivariant and proper maps.

Let
X
be a
G
-set with a
G
-coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
and a
G
-bornology
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
.

Definition 2.9
.

{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
are compatible if for every
B
in
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
and every
V
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
we have
V[B]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
.

Definition 2.10
.

A
G
-bornological coarse space is a triple of a
G
-set
X
with a
G
-coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
and a
G
-bornology
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
such that
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
are compatible.
A morphism between
G
-bornological coarse spaces is a morphism of
G
-sets which is proper and controlled.

In this way we get a category
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
of
G
-bornological coarse spaces and morphisms.
We usually denote a
G
-bornological coarse space

(X,{\mathcal{C}}_{X},{\mathcal{B}}_{X})
just by the symbol
X
.

Example 2.11
.

If
(X,d)
is a metric space with an isometric action of
G
, then we get a
G
-bornological coarse space
X_{d}
. Its
G
-coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}_{d}
is described in Example
2.4
. Its bornology
{\mathcal{B}}_{d}
is the smallest one which is compatible
with
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
. Explicitly,
{\mathcal{B}}_{d}
is the set of all metrically bounded subsets of
X
.

∎

Example 2.12
.

For
S
in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
we can consider the objects
S_{max,max}
,
S_{min,max}
and
S_{min,min}
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
, where
the first index
min
or
max
refers to the minimal or maximal
G
-coarse structure (subsets of
{\mathtt{diag}}(S)
or all of
\mathcal{P}_{S\times S}
),
and the second
min
or
max
refers to the minimal or maximal bornology (all finite subets or all of
\mathcal{P}_{S}
) on
S
.

If
S
is infinite, then
S_{max,min}
does not exist since in this case the coarse structure and the bornology are not compatible.
∎

Example 2.13
.

The group
G
has a canonical
G
-coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}_{can}
generated by the entourages
\{(g,g^{\prime})\}
for all pairs of elements
g,g^{\prime}
in
G
. We
let
G_{can,min}
be the object of
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
given by the
G
-set
G
(with the left action) with the coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}_{can}
and the minimal bornology
{\mathcal{B}}_{min}
which consists of the finite subsets.

∎

Example 2.14
.

If
f\colon Z\to X
is a map in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
and
X
is in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
, then
Z
has an induced
G
-bornological coarse structure.
The coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}_{Z}
is the maximal one such that
f
is controlled, and the bornology
{\mathcal{B}}_{Z}
is the minimal one such that
f
is proper. These structures are in fact compatible. If
Z
is equipped with these structures, then
f
becomes a morphism in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
.

∎

The category
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
has a symmetric monoidal structure
\otimes
:

Definition 2.15

(
[
BEKW20b
, Ex. 2.17]
)
.

1.

For
X
,
X^{\prime}
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
the underlying
G
-set of
X\otimes X^{\prime}
is
X\times X^{\prime}
with the diagonal action. Its
G
-coarse structure is generated by the entourages
U\times U^{\prime}
for all
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
and
U^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X^{\prime}}
, and its
G
-bornology is generated by the sets
B\times B^{\prime}
for all
B
in
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
and
B^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{X}
.

2.

The tensor product of two morphisms
f,f^{\prime}
is given by the map
f\times f
between the cartesian products of the underlying
G
-sets.

3.

The tensor unit is the one-point space
*
with the unique bonological coarse structure.

4.

The associativity and symmetry constraints are imported from the cartesian structure on the category
G{\mathbf{Set}}
.

Let
f_{0},f_{1}\colon X\to X^{\prime}
be a pair of morphisms in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
.

Definition 2.16
.

f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are close to each other if
(f_{0}\times f_{1})({\mathtt{diag}}(X))\in{\mathcal{C}}_{X^{\prime}}
.

Example 2.17
.

We consider
{\mathbb{R}}
with the metric coarse structure.

The morphisms
{\mathtt{id}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}
and
x\mapsto-x
are not close to each other.

The morphisms
{\mathtt{id}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}
and
x\mapsto x+1
are close to each other.

∎

Let
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
be a morphism in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
.

Definition 2.18
.

1.

f
is a coarse equivalence if there exists a morphism
g\colon X^{\prime}\to X
such that
f\circ g
is close to
{\mathtt{id}}_{X^{\prime}}
and
g\circ f
is close to
{\mathtt{id}}_{X}
.

2.

f
is a weak coarse equivalence
it it becomes a coarse equivalence after forgetting the
G
-action.

Note that a coarse equivalence is a weak coarse equivalence, but the converse is in general not true:

Example 2.19
.

We consider the group
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
. We further consider the metric space
{\mathbb{R}}
as a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
-bornological coarse space such that the non-trivial element of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
acts as multiplication by
-1
. It preserves the subspace
{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
.
The inclusion
{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a morphism in
({\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}})\mathbf{BornCoarse}
which is a weak coarse equivalence, but not a coarse equivalence. Indeed, there does not exist any equivariant map
{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
, because the image of
0
under such a map had to be a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
-fixed point in the target which does not exist.

∎

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
.

Definition 2.20
.

X
is called flasque if it admits an endomorphism
f
such that:

1.

{\mathtt{id}}_{X}
and
f
are close to each other.

2.

For every
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
we have
\bigcup_{n\in{\rm Nature}}(f^{n}\times f^{n})(U)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
.

3.

For every
B
in
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
there exists
n
in
{\rm Nature}
such that
f^{n}(X)\cap B=\emptyset
.

We say that
f
implements the flasqueness of
X
.

Example 2.21
.

We consider
{\rm Nature}
with the bornological coarse structure induced by the usual metric.
Then
{\rm Nature}\otimes X
is flasque for
every
G
-bornological coarse space
X
. The selfmap
(n,x)\mapsto(n+1,x)
implements flasqueness.
∎

If
X
is in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
and
U
is in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}^{G}
, then we let
X_{U}
denote the object
(X,{\mathcal{C}}\langle\{U\}\rangle,{\mathcal{B}}_{X})
of
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
, where
{\mathcal{C}}\langle\{U\}\rangle
is the
G
-coarse structure generated by
U
. The identity of the underlying set of
X
induces a morphism
X_{U}\to X
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
.

Example 2.22
.

If
(X,d)
is a path metric space and we consider

U_{r}\coloneqq\{(x,x^{\prime})\in X\times X\>|\>d(x,x^{\prime})\leq r\}

for some
r
in
(0,\infty)
, then
X_{U_{r}}\to X_{d}
is an isomorphism (see Example
2.11
).

If we consider the subset
X\coloneqq\{n^{2}\>|\>n\in{\rm Nature}\}
of
{\mathbb{R}}
with the induced bornological coarse structure (Example
2.14
), then the map
X_{U}\to X
for
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
is never an isomorphism.

∎

Let
{\mathcal{Y}}\coloneqq(Y_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of subsets of
X
indexed by a very small filtered poset.

Definition 2.23
.

{\mathcal{Y}}
is an invariant big family if:

1.

For all
i,i^{\prime}
in
I
with
i\leq i^{\prime}
we have
Y_{i}\subseteq Y_{i^{\prime}}
.

2.

Y_{i}
is
G
-invariant for every
i
in
I
.

3.

For every
i
in
I
and
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
there exists
i^{\prime}
in
I
such that
U[Y_{i}]\subseteq Y_{i^{\prime}}
.

Example 2.24
.

In the case of the trivial group
the bornology
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
considered as a self-indexing family is an example of a big family on
X
by the compatibility of
{\mathcal{B}}
with the coarse structure.

In general, if
Y
is a
G
-invariant subset of
X
, then the family
\{Y\}:=(U[Y])_{U\in{\mathcal{C}}_{X}^{G}}
is an invariant big family. It is called the invariant big family generated by
Y
.

∎

Definition 2.25
.

An invariant complementary pair
({\mathcal{Y}},Z)
on
X
is a pair of an invariant big family
{\mathcal{Y}}
and an invariant subset
Z
of
X
such that there exists
i
in
I
with
Y_{i}\cup Z=X
.

If not said differently we consider subsets of
X
with the induced bornological coarse structures (Example
2.14
).
We can form the big family
{\mathcal{Y}}\cap Z\coloneqq(Y_{i}\cap Z)_{i\in I}
on
Z
.

In the present paper we use the language of
\infty
-categories
2
2

2

more precisely
(\infty,1)
-categories
. References are
[
Lur09
,
Cis19
]
. Ordinary categories will be considered as
\infty
-categories using the nerve functor.
A typical target
\infty
-category for the homological functors introduced below is the stable
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Sp}
of spectra. We refer to
[
Lur
]
for an introduction to stable
\infty
-categories in general, and for
\mathbf{Sp}
in particular.
The
\infty
-categories considered in the present paper belong to the large universe.

Let

E\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to\mathbf{M}

be a functor with target some
\infty
-category. For an invariant big family
{\mathcal{Y}}=(Y_{i})_{i\in I}
on some
G
bornological coarse space
X
we set

E({\mathcal{Y}})\coloneqq\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I}E(Y_{i})

provided this colimit exists.

Definition 2.26
.

E
is coarsely invariant if for any pair of close morphism
f_{0},f_{1}\colon X\to X^{\prime}
the induced morphisms
E(f_{0})
and
E(f_{1})
are equivalent.

One can show that
E
is coarsely invariant if and only if it sends coarse equivalences to equivalences.

Definition 2.27
.

E
is excisive, if the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

E(\emptyset)
is an initial object in
\mathbf{M}
.

2.

For every
X
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
and invariant complementary pair
({\mathcal{Y}},Z)
on
X
:

(a)

E({\mathcal{Y}})
and
E({\mathcal{Y}}\cap Z)
exist.

(b)

The square

\textstyle{E({\mathcal{Y}}\cap Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E({\mathcal{Y}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(X)}

is a push-out square in
\mathbf{M}
.

Definition 2.28
.

E
is
u
-continuous if for every
X
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

The colimit
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{U\in{\mathcal{C}}_{X}^{G}}E(X_{U})
exists.

2.

The canonical morphism

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{U\in{\mathcal{C}}_{X}^{G}}E(X_{U})\to E(X)
is an equivalence.

Definition 2.29
.

E
vanishes on flasques if for every flasque object
X
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
the object
E(X)
is initial in
\mathbf{M}
.

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
and
L
be a subset of
X
.

Definition 2.30
.

We say that
L
is locally finite if
|L\cap B|<\infty
for all
B
in
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
.

We let
\mathrm{LF}(X)
denote the poset of locally finite,
G
-invariant subsets of
X
. If
f\colon X\to Y
is a morphism in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
, then it induces a morphism of posets
\mathrm{LF}(X)\to\mathrm{LF}(Y)
given by
L\mapsto f(L)
.

Definition 2.31
.

E
is continuous if for every
X
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

The colimit
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{L\in\mathrm{LF}(X)}E(L)
exists.

2.

The canonical morphism
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{L\in\mathrm{LF}(X)}E(L)\to E(X)
is an equivalence.

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
, and let
Y,Z
be two subsets.

Definition 2.32
.

Y
and
Z
are coarsely disjoint if
U[Y]\cap U[Z]=\emptyset
for every
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
.

We assume that
\mathbf{M}
is pointed and let
0_{\mathbf{M}}
denote the zero-object.

Definition 2.33
.

E
is called
\pi_{0}
-excisive if for every
X
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
and invariant coarsely disjoint partition
(Y,Z)
of
X
into
G
-invariant subsets the square

\textstyle{0_{\mathbf{M}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(X)}

(2.1)

is a push-out square.

Example 2.34
.

An excisive functor is
\pi_{0}
-excisive. In fact, if
(Y,Z)
is any invariant coarsely disjoint partition of
X
into
G
-invariant subsets, then
(Z,(Y))
is an equivariant complementary pair, where
(Y)
is the single member family with member
Y
.
Moreover,
Z\cap(Y)=(\emptyset)
. In view of Definition
2.27
the assertion is now obvious.

∎

We consider an invariant coarsely disjoint partition
(Y,Z)
of
X
into
G
-invariant subsets.
The pair of maps
{\mathtt{id}}_{E(Y)}\colon E(Y)\to E(Y)
and
0\colon E(Z)\to 0_{\mathbf{M}}\to E(Y)
induce, using the presentation of
E(X)
as a push-out (
2.1
), a projection
p_{Y}\colon E(X)\to E(Y)
.

Let
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
indexed by a very small set
I
.

Definition 2.35

(
[
BEKW20b
, Ex. 2.16]
)
.

The free union
\bigsqcup^{\mathrm{free}}_{i\in I}X_{i}
is the following object of
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
:

1.

The underlying
G
-set is the disjoint union
\bigsqcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
of
G
-sets.

2.

The
G
-coarse structure is generated by the entourages
\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
for all families
(U_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\prod_{i\in I}{\mathcal{C}}_{X_{i}}
.

3.

The
G
-bornology is generated by
\bigcup_{i\in I}{\mathcal{B}}_{X_{i}}
.

For every
j
in
I
we have an invariant and coarsely disjoint decomposition

(X_{j},\bigsqcup^{\mathrm{free}}_{i\in I\setminus\{j\}}X_{i})
of
\bigsqcup^{\mathrm{free}}_{i\in I}X_{i}
.
If
E
is
\pi_{0}
-excisive, then we have a projection

p_{j}\colon E\big(\bigsqcup^{\mathrm{free}}_{i\in I}X_{i}\big)\to E(X_{j})\,.

Definition 2.36

(
[
BEKW20b
, Def. 3.12]
)
.

E
is called strongly additive, if:

1.

\mathbf{M}
is pointed.

2.

E
is
\pi_{0}
-excisive.

3.

For every family
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
indexed by a very small set
I
we have:

(a)

The product
\prod_{j\in I}E(X_{j})
exists.

(b)

The canonical morphism

(p_{j})_{j\in I}\colon E(\bigsqcup^{\mathrm{free}}_{i\in I}X_{i})\to\prod_{j\in I}E(X_{j})
is an equivalence.

We consider a functor
E\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to\mathbf{M}
where
\mathbf{M}
is a stable
\infty
-category.

Definition 2.37
.

E
is an equivariant coarse homology theory if it has the following properties:

1.

E
coarsely invariant

2.

E
is excisive.

3.

E
is
u
-continuous.

4.

E
vanishes on flasques.

Note that the target category of an equivariant coarse homology theory is always assumed to be stable.
Usually it will also be cocomplete. In this case the conditions of existence of various filtered colimits in the definitions above
are automatic.

The properties of continuity and strong additivity are considered as additional properties which
a coarse homology may or may not have.

Example 2.38
.

If
Y
is in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
and
E\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to\mathbf{M}
is an equivariant coarse homology theory, then we can define a new equivariant coarse homology theory

E_{Y}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to\mathbf{M}\,,\quad X\mapsto E(Y\otimes X)

called the twist of
E
by
Y
, see
[
BEKW20b
, Sec. 4.3]
. Here
\otimes
is the symmetric monoidal structure on
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
introduced in Definition
2.15
.

∎

Recall the Definition
2.20
flasquenes. In order to obtain the notion of weak flasqueness
we weaken the Condition
2.20
.
1
. Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
.

Definition 2.39

(
[
BEKW20b
, Def. 4.18]
)
.

X
is called weakly flasque if it admits an endomorphism
f
such that:

1.

For every equivariant coarse homology theory
E
we have
E({\mathtt{id}}_{X})\simeq E(f)
.

2.

For every
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
we have
\bigcup_{n\in{\rm Nature}}(f^{n}\times f^{n})(U)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
.

3.

For every
B
in
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
there exists
n
in
{\rm Nature}
such that
f^{n}(X)\cap B=\emptyset
.

We say that
f
implements weak flasqueness of
X
.
Note that in
[
BEKW20b
, Def. 4.18]
we used the universal equivariant coarse homology theory
\mathrm{Yo}^{s}
to give an equivalent formulation of Condition
2.39
.
1
.

Since an equivariant coarse homology theory is coarsely invariant it is clear that a flasque
G
-bornological coarse space is weakly flasque.

Let
E\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to\mathbf{M}
be an equivariant coarse homology theory.

Definition 2.40
.

E
is called strong if it annihilates weakly flasque
G
-bornological coarse spaces.

Strongness of a coarse homology theory plays an important role in the construction and study of the coarse assembly map
[
BE20a
]

and its equivariant generalization
[
BEKW20b
, Sec. 11.3]
.
All examples of coarse homology theories constructed in the present paper are strong.

3
C^{*}
-categories and homological functors

In this section we recall the basic definitions and facts on
C^{*}
-categories which will be used in the present paper.
For details and further references we refer to
[
BEa
]
and
[
Bun
]
.
We start with a description of the category
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of possibly non-unital
C^{*}
-categories.
A
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear
*
-category is a possibly non-unital (we do not require the existence of identity endomorphisms) category
{\mathbf{C}}
which is enriched in complex vector spaces and has an antilinear involution
*\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
fixing objects. Note that a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
is an example of a
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
-linear
C^{*}
-category with a single object. A morphism between
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear
*
-categories is a not necessarily unit-preserving functor which is compatible with the enrichment and the involutions. We let
\mathbf{{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}}
denote the large category of small
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear
*
-categories and morphisms. By
\mathbf{{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}}
we denote its subcategory of unital
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear
*
-categories (where all objects admit identity morphism) and unit-preserving functors.

Given a morphism
f
in a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear
*
-category
{\mathbf{C}}
we can define its maximal seminorm

\|f\|_{\max}
in
[0,\infty]
as the supremum of the norms
\|\rho(f)\|
for all morphisms
\rho\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to A
from
{\mathbf{C}}
to
C^{*}
-algebras
A
. A
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear
*
-category is called a pre-
C^{*}
-category if all its morphisms have finite maximal seminorms.

Definition 3.1
.

A
C^{*}
-category is a pre-
C^{*}
-category with the property that its
{\mathtt{Hom}}
-spaces are Banach spaces
with respect to the maximal norm. A morphism between
C^{*}
-categories is a morphism of
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear
*
-categories.

By definition,
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is a full subcategory of
\mathbf{{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}}
.
In order to show that a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear
*
-category
{\mathbf{C}}
is a
C^{*}
-category it suffices
to provide a submultiplicative Banach space norm on the morphism spaces which in addition satisfies the
C^{*}
-identity and has the property that

f^{*}\circ f
is a non-negative element in the corresponding endomorphism
C^{*}
-algebra for any morphism
f
in
{\mathbf{C}}
.
We say that this norm exhibits
{\mathbf{C}}
as a
C^{*}
-category.
When we talk about morphisms in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
we will use the words morphism or functor synonymously.
In this case it coincides with the maximal seminorm.
A functor between
C^{*}
-categories automatically induces contractions on the morphism spaces.

Definition 3.2
.

A functor between
C^{*}
-categories is said to be faithful (fully faithful) if it induces injections (bijections)
on morphism spaces.

Note that a faithful functor induces isometries on the morphism spaces.

We have inclusions
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\subseteq C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}\subseteq C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}

of the categories of
C^{*}
-algebras and unital
C^{*}
-algebras into the corresponding categories of
C^{*}
-categories which interpret
C^{*}
-algebras as single object
C^{*}
-categories.
The following generalizes the notion of an exact sequence of
C^{*}
-algebras and of an ideal to
C^{*}
-categories.

Definition 3.3
.

A sequence of functors
{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{E}}
between
C^{*}
-categories will be called exact
if the functors are bijections on the level of objects and induce short exact sequences on the level of morphism spaces.

We will usually indicate exactness by writing
0\to{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{E}}\to 0
.

Definition 3.4
.

A functor
{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
between
C^{*}
-categories is an ideal inclusion if it faithful, a bijection on the level of objects, and if the composition of a morphism from
{\mathbf{D}}
with a morphism from
{\mathbf{C}}
always belongs to
{\mathbf{C}}
.

In an exact sequence as above the inclusion
{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is an ideal inclusion, and every ideal inclusion
can be extended to an exact sequence by adding the quotient
C^{*}
-category as the third term.

For a group
G
we let
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
denote the category of
C^{*}
-categories with a strict
G
-action.
There are a maximal and a reduced crossed product functor

-\times G,-\rtimes_{r}G\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}

described in
[
Bun
, Sec. 5]
and
[
BEa
, Sec. 12]
, respectively.

Example 3.5
.

We consider a very small
C^{*}
-algebra with
G
-action

A
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
. We write the action of
g
in
G
on
A
by
a\mapsto{}^{g}a
.

Following
[
BEa
, Ex. 2.10]
we have the
C^{*}
-category with
G
-action

\mathbf{Hilb}(A)
of very small
A
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
.
The objects of
\mathbf{Hilb}(A)
are the very small
A
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules, and the morphisms are the adjointable bounded operators between Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules. If
g
is an element of
G
, then the functor

g\colon\mathbf{Hilb}(A)\to\mathbf{Hilb}(A)
is defined as follows:

1.

objects: We consider an
A
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-module
M
with right multiplication
\cdot
and
A
-valued scalar product
\langle-,-\rangle_{M}
. The underlying complex vector space of
gM
is that of
M
.
The right
A
-action
\cdot_{g}
on
gM
is given by
m\cdot_{g}a\coloneqq m\cdot{}^{g^{-1}}a
, and the
A
-valued scalar product
is
\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle_{gM}\coloneqq{}^{g}\langle m,m^{\prime}\rangle_{M}
.

2.

For a morphism
f\colon M\to M^{\prime}
the morphism
gf\colon gM\to gM^{\prime}
is given by the same linear map
f
.

The
C^{*}
-category
\mathbf{Hilb}(A)
is unital, but it has an invariant non-unital subcategory
\mathbf{Hilb}_{c}(A)

with the same objects but the compact operators (in the sense of
A
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules) as morphisms.
The inclusion
\mathbf{Hilb}_{c}(A)\to\mathbf{Hilb}(A)
is an ideal inclusion.

∎

We can take the
C^{*}
-category
\mathbf{Hilb}_{c}(A)
described in Example
3.5
as the coefficient category for the constructions in the subsequent sections which then produce a variety of new examples
of
C^{*}
-categories with and without
G
-action.

For the following material we refer to
[
BEa
, Sec. 2 & Sec. 3]
.
The notion of a
W^{*}
-category generalizes that of a von Neumann algebra.

Definition 3.6
.

A
W^{*}
-category is a unital
C^{*}
-category with the property that all its morphism Banach spaces admit pre-duals.

By
[
BEa
, Def. 2.35]
, to any
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbf{C}}
one can functorially associate its
W^{*}
-envelope
{\mathbf{W}}^{\mathrm{nu}}{\mathbf{C}}
. The superscript indicates that it is the non-unital version of the functor
{\mathbf{W}}
from unital
C^{*}
-categories to
W^{*}
-categories. The latter is characterized as the left-adjoint to the inclusion from unital
W^{*}
-categories (and functors which are in addition
\sigma
-weakly continuous) to unital
C^{*}
-categories.
For a non-unital
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbf{C}}
we then get the
W^{*}
-catergory
{\mathbf{W}}^{\mathrm{nu}}{\mathbf{C}}
(it is unital) as the
\sigma
-weak closure of
{\mathbf{C}}
under

{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{u}\to{\mathbf{W}}{\mathbf{C}}^{u}
, where
{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{u}
denotes the unitalization.
We have an isometric and
\sigma
-weakly dense inclusion

{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{W}}^{\mathrm{nu}}{\mathbf{C}}
, and if
f\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a morphism of
C^{*}
-categories, then the functor
{\mathbf{W}}^{\mathrm{nu}}(f)\colon{\mathbf{W}}^{\mathrm{nu}}{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{W}}^{\mathrm{nu}}{\mathbf{D}}
is in addition

\sigma
-weakly continuous.

In order to give a quick description of the multiplier category of a
C^{*}
-category we turn
[
BEa
, Thm. 3.15]
into a definition.

Definition 3.7
.

The multiplier category
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
is defined as the wide subcategory of
{\mathbf{W}}^{\mathrm{nu}}{\mathbf{C}}
which idealizes
{\mathbf{C}}
.

The multiplier category
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
thus consists of those morphisms of
{\mathbf{W}}^{\mathrm{nu}}{\mathbf{C}}
which have the property that all compositions with morphisms from
{\mathbf{C}}
again belong to
{\mathbf{C}}
. Note that
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
is unital and contains
{\mathbf{C}}
as an ideal.
The universal property of the inclusion
{\mathbf{C}}\to\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
says that it is a final object in the category of ideal inclusions under
{\mathbf{C}}
.

Definition 3.8
.

A morphism
f\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is non-degenerate if for any two objects
C,C^{\prime}
of
{\mathbf{C}}

the linear subspaces
\phi({\mathtt{End}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}(C^{\prime})){\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(\phi(C),\phi(C^{\prime}))
and

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(\phi(C),\phi(C^{\prime}))\phi({\mathtt{End}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}(C))
are dense in
{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{D}}}(\phi(C),\phi(C^{\prime}))
.

If
f\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is non-degenerate, then by
[
BEa
, Prop. 3.16]

the morphism
{\mathbf{W}}^{\mathrm{nu}}(f)\colon{\mathbf{W}}^{\mathrm{nu}}{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{W}}^{\mathrm{nu}}{\mathbf{D}}
restricts to a morphism
\mathbf{M}(f)\colon\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}\to\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{D}}
.

Example 3.9
.

In the situation of the Example
3.5
(forget the
G
-action) we have an isomorphism

\mathbf{M}\mathbf{Hilb}_{c}(A)\cong\mathbf{Hilb}(A)
.
If
A\to B
is a homomorphism, then the functor
-\otimes_{A}B\colon\mathbf{Hilb}_{c}(A)\to\mathbf{Hilb}_{c}(B)
is a non-degenerate morphism.
Its extension to the multiplier categories corresponds under the isomorphism above to the same functor
-\otimes_{A}B\colon\mathbf{Hilb}(A)\to\mathbf{Hilb}(B)
.
∎

Definition 3.10
.

An endomorphism
p
in a
C^{*}
-category is called a projection if
p=p^{*}=p^{2}
.

Definition 3.11
.

A morphism
u\colon C\to C^{\prime}
is called a unitary (an isometry, or a partial isometry), if

u^{*}\circ u={\mathtt{id}}_{C}
and
u\circ u^{*}={\mathtt{id}}_{C^{\prime}}
(
u^{*}\circ u={\mathtt{id}}_{C}
, or
u\circ u^{*}
and
u\circ u^{*}
are projections, respectively).

Note that objects supporting a unitary or an isometry are necessarily unital.

Definition 3.12
.

A projection
p
on
C
is effective if there exists an isometry
u\colon C^{\prime}\to C
with
u\circ u^{*}=p
.
We then say that
(C^{\prime},u)
represents an image of
p
.

Definition 3.13
.

We say that a unital
C^{*}
-category is idempotent complete if every projection is effective.

Consider a finite collection
(C_{i})_{i\in I}
of objects in a
C^{*}
-category.

Definition 3.14
.

An orthogonal sum of this family is a pair
(C,(e_{i})_{i\in I})
of an object
C
and a family of isometries

e_{i}\colon C_{i}\to C
such that

\sum_{i\in I}e_{i}\circ e_{i}^{*}={\mathtt{id}}_{C}\,.

(3.1)

Again note that in this case the objects

C
and
C_{i}
for all
i
in
I
are necessarily unital.

Definition 3.15
.

We say that
C^{*}
-category is additive if it admits orthogonal sums for all finite families of objects.

An additive
C^{*}
-category is unital.

The category
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
of unital
C^{*}
-categories and unit-preserving functors has a natural extension to a
(2,1)
-category
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{2,1}
whose
2
-isomorphisms are natural transformations (called unitary isomorphisms) implemented by unitaries.

To any unital
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbf{C}}
we can functorially associate its
additive and its additive and idempotent completions

{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}_{\oplus}\to\mathrm{Idem}({\mathbf{C}}_{\oplus})\,,

see
[
BEa
, Sec. 16]
.
These constructions are left-adjoints of two-categorical adjunctions whose right-adjoints are the respective inclusion functors.

Definition 3.16
.

A unital morphism
{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
between unital
C^{*}
-categories is called a Morita equivalence if it is fully faithful and
the induced morphism
\mathrm{Idem}({\mathbf{C}}_{\oplus})\to\mathrm{Idem}({\mathbf{D}}_{\oplus})
is an equivalence.

In order to check whether a fully faithful morphism
{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a Morita equivalence it suffices to
show that every object of
{\mathbf{D}}
is a subobject of a finite sum of objects of
{\mathbf{C}}
.

Example 3.17
.

The
C^{*}
-categories
\mathbf{Hilb}(A)
and the full subcategories
\mathbf{Hilb}^{\mathrm{fg},\mathrm{free}}(A)
and
\mathbf{Hilb}(A)^{\mathrm{fg},\mathrm{proj}}

of finitely generated free or finitely generated projective modules, respectively, are additive. In general,

\mathbf{Hilb}_{c}(A)
is not additive.

The categories
\mathbf{Hilb}(A)
and the full subcategory
\mathbf{Hilb}(A)^{\mathrm{fg},\mathrm{proj}}
are
idempotent complete, but
\mathbf{Hilb}^{\mathrm{fg},\mathrm{free}}(A)
in general is not. The inclusion

\mathbf{Hilb}^{\mathrm{fg},\mathrm{free}}(A)\to\mathbf{Hilb}^{\mathrm{fg},\mathrm{proj}}(A)
presents the target as an idempotent completion.
∎

In order to extend the definition of an orthogonal sum to infinite families
we must specify in which sense the sum in (
3.1
) converges.
Requiring convergence in norm would exclude all non-trivial examples of infinite sums.
In the present paper we will work with the notion of AV-sums (named after
[
AV20
]
where this definition has been introduced). We will use the strict topology on the morphism spaces of
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
which is the topology of pointwise convergence
of compositions with morphisms in
{\mathbf{C}}
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be a
C^{*}
-category and
consider a small family

(C_{i})_{i\in I}
of objects in
{\mathbf{C}}
.

Definition 3.18
.

An orthogonal AV-sum
of this family is a pair

(C,(e_{i})_{i\in I})
of an object
C
and a family of isometries

e_{i}\colon C_{i}\to C
in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
such that the sum in (
3.1
) converges
in the strict topology.

We refer to
[
BEa
, Sec. 7]
for a description of the space of morphisms from and into AV-sums.
If
\phi\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a non-degenerate morphism, then it preserves AV-sums.

Example 3.19
.

AV-sums in
\mathbf{Hilb}_{c}(A)
are represented by orthogonal sums of
A
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules in the usual sense, see
[
BEa
, Sec. 8]
.
∎

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be a
C^{*}
-category.

Definition 3.20
.

We call
{\mathbf{C}}
effectively additive if for every object
C
of
{\mathbf{C}}
and very small mutually orthogonal family of effective projections
(p_{i})_{i\in I}
on
C
in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
such that
\sum_{i\in I}p_{i}
converges strictly to a projection
p
in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
also
p
is effective in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
.

Thus
{\mathbf{C}}
is effectively additive if it admits orthogonal AV-sums of orthogonal families of subobjects of objects.
If
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
is idempotent complete, then it is clearly effectively additive.
If
{\mathbf{C}}
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
admits all very small orthogonal AV-sums, then it is effectively additive
by
[
BEa
, Lem. 7.9]
.

We introduce the following notation:

Definition 3.21
.

1.

C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{ndeg}}
is the wide subcategory of
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of non-degenerate morphisms.

2.

C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{eadd,ndeg}}
is the full subcategory of
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{ndeg}}
of effectively additive objects.

3.

C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{\infty add,ndeg}}
is the full subcategory of
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{eadd,ndeg}}
of objects
admitting countable orthogonal AV-sums.

4.

C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sadd,ndeg}}
is the full subcategory of
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{\infty add,ndeg}}
of objects
admitting all very small orthogonal AV-sums.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be an additive
C^{*}
-category.
If
\phi,\psi\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
are two functors, then we can define an orthogonal sum
\phi\oplus\psi
by
choosing sums of
(\phi(C^{\prime}),\psi(C^{\prime}))
for any object
C^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
. The sum of functors is well-defined up to unitary isomorphism.

Definition 3.22

(
[
BEa
, Defn. 11.3]
)
.

{\mathbf{C}}
is called flasque if it admits an endofunctor
S\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}

such that there is a unitary isomorphism
{\mathtt{id}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}\oplus S\cong S
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}
admits countable AV-sums, then it is flasque by
[
BEa
, Ex. 11.5]
.

We consider a stable
\infty
-category
\mathbf{M}
and a functor
\mathrm{Hg}\colon C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathbf{M}
. For the following notions see also
[
BEa
, Sec. 13]
.

Definition 3.23
.

1.

The functor
\mathrm{Hg}
is called a homological functor if it sends unitary equivalences to equivalences and
exact sequences to fibre sequences.

2.

The functor
\mathrm{Hg}
is called finitary, if
\mathbf{M}
is cocomplete and
\mathrm{Hg}
in addition preserves very small filtered colimits.

3.

The functor
\mathrm{Hg}
is called Morita invariant if it sends Morita equivalences to equivalences.

A zero object in a
C^{*}
-category is an object whose endomorphism algebra is the zero algebra.
A zero category is a
C^{*}
-category with only zero objects. A homological functor sends zero categories to
zero.

A homological functor sends products of
C^{*}
-categories to sums and preserves sums of morphisms to an additive
C^{*}
-category. It in particular annihilates flasque
C^{*}
-categories.

Definition 3.24

(
[
BEa
, Def. 13.5]
)
.

A square of
C^{*}
-categories

\textstyle{{\mathbf{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{J}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}}

is called excisive if the horizontal functors are inclusions of ideals
and the induced map
{\mathbf{A}}/{\mathbf{I}}\to{\mathbf{B}}/\mathbf{J}
of quotients is a unitary equivalence between unital
C^{*}
-categories.

A homological functor sends excisive squares of
C^{*}
-categories to cocartesian squares.

Our basic example of a homological functor is the
K
-theory functor for
C^{*}
-categories

\mathrm{K^{C^{*}Cat}}\colon C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathbf{Sp}\,,

(3.2)

see
[
BEa
, Sec. 14]
.
This functor is finitary, Morita invariant, and in addition it preserves arbitrary products of additive
C^{*}
-categories by
[
BEa
, Thm. 15.7]
.

4
Controlled objects

In this section we introduce the
C^{*}
-category of objects in a coefficient
C^{*}
-category with
G
-action which are controlled over a
G
-set.

Let
G
be a very small group. For any category
{\mathcal{C}}
we let
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,{\mathcal{C}})
denote the category of
G
-objects in
{\mathcal{C}}
and equivariant morphisms.
To any
{\mathbf{D}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})

we will associate
the functor

{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(-)\colon G{\mathbf{Set}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}

(4.1)

which associates to
X
in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
the unital
C^{*}
-category of equivariant
X
-controlled objects in
{\mathbf{D}}
and equivariant multiplier morphisms.
We will apply this construction to

{\mathbf{D}}=\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
for
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
.
Provided that
{\mathbf{C}}
is effectively additive we will furthermore define a full subfunctor

{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}^{G}(-)\colon G{\mathbf{Set}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}

(4.2)

of
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(-)
such that
{\mathbf{C}}^{G}_{\mathrm{pt}}(X)
is the full subcategory of those objects which are determined on points.

We first introduce the
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}
of
G
-objects in

{\mathbf{D}}
.

Definition 4.1
.

1.

A
G
-object in
{\mathbf{D}}
is a pair
(D,\rho)
of an object
D
in
{\mathbf{D}}
and a family of unitary morphisms
\rho\coloneqq(\rho_{g})_{g\in G}
,
\rho_{g}\colon D\to gD
such that
for all
g,h
in
G
we have
g\rho_{h}\circ\rho_{g}=\rho_{gh}
.

2.

A morphism of
G
-objects
(D,\rho)\to(D^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
is a
morphism
A\colon D\to D^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{D}}
such that for every
g
in
G
we have

gA\circ\rho_{g}=\rho^{\prime}_{g}\circ A
. The
{\mathbb{C}}
-vector space structure on the morphism sets is inherited from
{\mathbf{D}}
.

3.

We let
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
denote the
C^{*}
-category of
G
-objects in
{\mathbf{D}}
and corresponding morphisms. The composition and the involution on
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}
are inherited from
{\mathbf{D}}
.

Remark 4.2
.

In this remark we explain why
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}
is well-defined. Note that Condition
4.1
.
2
on
A
is linear and compatible with the composition and involution. This implies that
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}
is a well-defined object of
\mathbf{{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}}
. We have a forgetful functor

{\mathbf{D}}^{G}\to{\mathbf{D}}
which sends
(D,\rho)
to
D
and acts as the natural inclusion on morphism spaces.
Since the Condition
4.1
.
2
is also continuous in
A
we can conclude that
the restriction of the norm of
{\mathbf{D}}
to
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}
exhibits the latter as a
C^{*}
-category.

∎

The construction of the category of
G
-objects is functorial. Let
\phi\colon{\mathbf{D}}_{0}\to{\mathbf{D}}_{1}
be a functor in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})
.
Then we get an induced functor
\phi^{G}\colon{\mathbf{D}}_{0}^{G}\to{\mathbf{D}}_{1}^{G}
as follows:

1.

objects: The functor
\phi^{G}
sends the object
(D,\rho)
in
{\mathbf{D}}_{0}^{G}
to the object
(\phi(D),\phi(\rho))
in
{\mathbf{D}}_{1}^{G}
, where
\phi(\rho)\coloneqq(\phi(\rho_{g}))_{g\in G}
.

2.

morphisms: The functor
\phi^{G}
sends a morphism
A\colon(D,\rho)\to(D^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
to the morphism
\phi(A)\colon(\phi(D),\phi(\rho))\to(\phi(D^{\prime}),\phi(\rho^{\prime}))
.

One checks that
\phi^{G}
is well-defined, and that
\phi\mapsto\phi^{G}
is compatible with compositions.
We therefore obtain a functor

(-)^{G}\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\,.

(4.3)

Let
{\mathbf{D}}
be in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})
. If
D
is an object of
{\mathbf{D}}
, then
\mathrm{Proj}(D)
denotes the (small) set of selfadjoint projections on
D
. Let
X
be in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
.

Definition 4.3
.

An equivariant
X
-controlled object in
{\mathbf{D}}
is
a triple
(D,\rho,\mu)
of the following objects:

1.

(D,\rho)
is an object of
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}
.

2.

\mu
is a function
\mu\colon\mathcal{P}_{X}\to\mathrm{Proj}(D)
.

The triple must have the following properties:

3.

\mu
is
G
-equivariant, i.e., we have

\mu(gY)=\rho_{g}^{-1}\circ g\mu(Y)\circ\rho_{g}

(4.4)

for all
Y
in
\mathcal{P}_{X}
and
g
in
G
.

4.

\mu
is finitely additive, i.e.,
\mu(\emptyset)=0
and
for all subsets
Y,Z
of
X

with
Y\subseteq Z
we have

\mu(Z)=\mu(Y)+\mu(Y\setminus Z)
.

5.

\mu
is full, i.e.,

\mu(X)={\mathtt{id}}_{D}
.

Remark 4.4
.

Note that Condition
4.3
.
4
implies that for all subsets
Y,Y^{\prime}
of
X
we have
[\mu(Y),\mu(Y^{\prime})]=0
.

∎

Let
X
be in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
, and let
{{{\mathbf{D}}}}
be in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})
.

Definition 4.5
.

We define
the
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(X)
as follows:

1.

objects: The objects of
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(X)
are equivariant
X
-controlled objects
(D,\rho,\mu)
in
{\mathbf{D}}
.

2.

morphisms: A morphism
A\colon(D,\rho,\mu)\to(D^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
in
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(X)
is a morphism
A\colon(D,\rho)\to(D^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
in
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}
. The
{\mathbb{C}}
-vector space structure is inherited from
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}
.

3.

The composition and the involution are inherited from
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}
.

It is obvious that
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(X)
belongs to
\mathbf{{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}}
. We have a forgetful functor
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(X)\to{\mathbf{D}}^{G}
which sends

(D,\rho,\mu)
to
(D,\rho)
and is the identity on morphisms. The restriction of the norm on
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}
along this functor exhibits

{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(X)
as a
C^{*}
-category.

Note that
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(X)
is actually a small category.

Example 4.6
.

The category
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(X)
may contain pathological objects.
For simplicity we consider the case of the trivial group.
Fix an ultrafilter
{\mathcal{U}}
on
X
.
If
D
is an object of
{\mathbf{D}}
, then we could consider the object

(D,\rho,\mu)
in
{\mathbf{D}}(X)
where

\mu(Y)\coloneqq\begin{cases}0&{\text{if }Y\not\in{\mathcal{U}}}\,,\\
{\mathtt{id}}_{D}&{\text{if }Y\in{\mathcal{U}}}\,.\end{cases}

In Definition
4.10
below we will introduce a condition which excludes such objects for free ultrafilters.

∎

Example 4.7
.

The category
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(\emptyset)
consists of zero objects.
Indeed, let
(D,\rho,\mu)
be in
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(\emptyset)
. Then
{\mathtt{id}}_{D}=\mu(\emptyset)=0
by
additivity and fullness.

∎

Assume that
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
is a morphism in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
.

Definition 4.8
.

We define a functor
f_{*}\colon{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(X)\to{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(X^{\prime})
as follows:

1.

objects:
f_{*}(D,\rho,\mu)\coloneqq(D,\rho,f_{*}\mu)
, where
f_{*}\mu\coloneqq\mu\circ f^{-1}
.

2.

morphisms:
f_{*}
acts as the identity.

It is clear that
f_{*}\mu
is again full and finitely additive. Since
f
is
G
-equivariant,
f_{*}\mu
is again
G
-equivariant.
Furthermore, the association
f\mapsto f_{*}
is compatible with compositions.

We fix
{\mathbf{D}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})
.

Definition 4.9
.

We define the functor
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(-)\colon G{\mathbf{Set}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
as follows:

1.

objects: The functor
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(-)
sends
X
in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
to the
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(X)
defined in the Definition
4.5
.

2.

morphisms: The functor
{\mathbf{D}}^{G}(-)
sends the morphism
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
to the functor
f_{*}
defined in Definition
4.8
.

We now consider
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
. Then we can apply the constructions above to the multiplier category
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})
.
Let
X
be in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
, and let
(C,\rho,\mu)
be in
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(X)
.

Recall the Definition
3.18
of an AV-sum.

Definition 4.10
.

The object
(C,\rho,\mu)
is determined on points if
C
is the orthogonal AV-sum of the images of the family of projections
(\mu(\{x\}))_{x\in X}
.

Explicitly this means that the projection
\mu(\{x\})
is effective (see Definition
3.12
) for every
x
in
X
and that
\sum_{x\in X}\mu(\{x\})
converges strictly to
{\mathtt{id}}_{C}
.
Note that except for degenerate cases the pathological object in Example
4.6
is not determined on points if the ultrafilter is free.

Definition 4.11
.

We define the
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbf{C}}^{G}_{\mathrm{pt}}(X)
as the full subcategory of
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(X)
of all objects which are determined on points.

Let
X
be in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
,
Y
be a subset of
X
, and let
(C,\rho,\mu)
be in
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(X)
.
Recall the Definition
3.20
of effective additivity. For categories with
G
-action we will apply this definition to the underlying
C^{*}
-category.

Lemma 4.12
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}
is effectively additive and

(C,\rho,\mu)
is determined on points, then
\mu(Y)
is effective.

Proof.

We have a mutually orthogonal family of effective projections
(\mu(\{x\}))_{x\in Y}
and
\sum_{x\in Y}\mu(\{x\})
strictly converges to
\mu(Y)
.
Therefore
\mu(Y)
is effective by our assumption on
{\mathbf{C}}
.
∎

Let
X
be in
G{\mathbf{Set}}

and let
(Y_{i})_{i\in I}
let be a be partition of
X
into subsets.
We consider

(C,\rho,\mu)
be in
{\mathbf{C}}^{G}_{\mathrm{pt}}(X)
.

Lemma 4.13
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}
is effectively additive, then

C
is the orthogonal AV-sum of the images of the family of projections
(\mu(Y_{i}))_{i\in I}
.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
4.12

the assumption implies that the projections
\mu(Y_{i})
are effective for all
i
in
I
. By
[
BEa
, Lem. 7.10]
we conclude that
C

an AV-sum of a family of images of the family
(\mu(Y_{i}))_{i\in I}
.
∎

We now show that the categories
{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}(X)
for
X
in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
assemble to a subfunctor of
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(-)
provided that
{\mathbf{C}}
is effectively additive.

Lemma 4.14
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}
is effectively additive, then
the morphism
f_{*}
in Definition
4.8
preserves the subcategories
of objects which are determined on points.

Proof.

Let
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
be a morphism in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
, and let
(C,\rho,\mu)
be an object in
{\mathbf{C}}^{G}_{\mathrm{pt}}(X)
.
We must show that
(C,\rho,f_{*}\mu)
is determined on points, i.e.,
that
C
is isomorphic to the sum of the images of the family of projections
((f_{*}\mu)(\{x^{\prime}\}))_{x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}}
.
But this follows from Lemma
4.13
applied to the partition
(f^{-1}(\{x^{\prime}\}))_{x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}}
of
X
.
∎

In view of Lemma
4.14
we can now make the following definition.
Recall that
{\mathbf{C}}
is in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
. In addition, we now assume that
{\mathbf{C}}
is effectively additive.

Definition 4.15
.

We define the functor

{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}^{G}(-)\colon G{\mathbf{Set}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}

as follows:

1.

objects: The functor
{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}^{G}(-)
sends
X
in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
to the
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}^{G}(X)
defined in Definition
4.11
.

2.

morphisms: The functor
{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}^{G}(-)
sends a morphism
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
to the restriction of the functor
f_{*}
defined in Definition
4.8
to the subcategories.

For fixed
X
in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
the constructions of
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(X)
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{G}_{\mathrm{pt}}(X)
are functorial for equivariant morphisms
\phi\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
of coefficient categories which are in addition non-degenerate.

We now employ the notation introduced in Definition
3.21
.
Let
\phi\colon{\mathbf{C}}_{0}\to{\mathbf{C}}_{1}
be a morphism in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{ndeg}})
. Then using (
4.3
) we
define a functor

\mathbf{M}\phi^{G}(X)\colon{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}_{0})}^{G}(X)\to{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}_{1})}^{G}(X)

(4.5)

as follows:

1.

objects: The functor
\mathbf{M}\phi^{G}(X)
sends the object
(C,\rho,\mu)
in
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}_{0})}^{G}(X)
to the object
(\phi(C),\mathbf{M}\phi(\rho),\mathbf{M}\phi(\mu))
in
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}_{1})}^{G}(X)
, where
\mathbf{M}\phi(\mu)(Y)\coloneqq\mathbf{M}\phi(\mu(Y))
for all subsets
Y
of
X
.

2.

morphisms: The functor
\mathbf{M}\phi^{G}(X)
sends a morphism
A\colon(C,\rho,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
in
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}_{0})}^{G}(X)
to the morphism

\mathbf{M}\phi(A)\colon(\phi(C),\mathbf{M}\phi(\rho),\mathbf{M}\phi(\mu))\to(\phi(C^{\prime}),\mathbf{M}\phi(\rho^{\prime}),\mathbf{M}\phi(\mu^{\prime}))

in
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}_{1})}^{G}(X)
.

We see that we actually have constructed a functor

G{\mathbf{Set}}\times{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{ndeg}})\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\,,\quad(X,{\mathbf{C}})\to{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(X)\,.

(4.6)

The restriction of this functor to
G{\mathbf{Set}}\times{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{eadd,ndeg}})

admits the subfunctor

G{\mathbf{Set}}\times{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{eadd,ndeg}})\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\,,\quad(X,{\mathbf{C}})\to{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}^{G}(X)\,.

(4.7)

5
Small objects and Roe categories

We consider
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
.

Assumption 5.1
.

As a standing hypothesis, when we talk about
{\mathbf{C}}^{G}_{\mathrm{pt}}
and objects derived from this like
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}
we will in addition assume that
{\mathbf{C}}
is effectively additive.
∎

Later we will assume in addition that
{\mathbf{C}}
admits countable AV-sums. Then for
X
in
G{\mathbf{Set}}

the
C^{*}
-categories
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(X)
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{G}_{\mathrm{pt}}(X)
are in general too large to be interesting from a
K
-theoretic point of view. Indeed, the condition on
{\mathbf{C}}
implies that
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(X)
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{G}_{\mathrm{pt}}(X)
also admit countable AV-sums and are therefore flasque, see Definition
3.22
and the subsequent text.
But flasque
C^{*}
-categories have trivial
K
-theoretic invariants by
[
BEa
, Prop. 13.13]
.

In this section we define subcategories of small objects
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
in
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(X)
, and
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}(X)
in
{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathrm{pt}}^{G}(X)
.
The notion of a small object depends on the choice of a
G
-bornology on
X
.

We have a forgetful functor
G\mathbf{Born}\to G{\mathbf{Set}}
which sends a
G
-bornological space to its underlying
G
-set (see Definition
2.8
). By precomposition with this forgetful functor we can consider the functors
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(-)
(Definition
4.9
) and
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{pt}}^{G}(-)
(Definition
4.15
) as functors on
G\mathbf{Born}
.
We will define a subfunctor

\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}\colon G\mathbf{Born}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\quad\text{of}\quad G\mathbf{Born}\xrightarrow{{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(-)}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\,.

Taking Assumption
5.1
into account we will furthermore define a subfunctor

\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}\colon G\mathbf{Born}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\quad\text{of}\quad G\mathbf{Born}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{pt}}^{G}(-)}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\,.

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{Born}
with bornology
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
, and
let
(C,\rho,\mu)
be an object of
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(X)
. Note that the
\mu
takes values in
{\mathtt{End}}_{\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}}(C)
, and that
{\mathtt{End}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}(C)
can be considered a subset of
{\mathtt{End}}_{\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}}(C)
.

Definition 5.2
.

(C,\rho,\mu)
is small if for every
B
in
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
we have
\mu(B)\in{\mathtt{End}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}(C)
.

Definition 5.3
.

We let
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
be the full subcategory of
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(X)
of small objects.

Let
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
be a morphism in
G\mathbf{Born}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

The morphism
f_{*}\colon\mathbf{C}^{G}(X)\to\mathbf{C}^{G}(X^{\prime})
preserves small objects.

Proof.

Let
(C,\rho,\mu)
be in
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
. We must check that
f_{*}(C,\rho,\mu)=(C,\rho,f_{*}\mu)
is small.
Let
B^{\prime}
be in
{\mathcal{B}}_{X^{\prime}}
. Then we have
f^{-1}(B)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
since
f
is proper. This implies that
(f_{*}\mu)(B^{\prime})=\mu(f^{-1}(B))\in{\mathtt{End}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}(C)
.
∎

Definition 5.5
.

We define the functor
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}\colon G\mathbf{Born}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
as the subfunctor of

{\mathbf{C}}^{G}(-)\colon G\mathbf{Born}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
which sends
X
in
G\mathbf{Born}
to the full subcategory
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
of small objects in
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}(X)
defined in Definition

5.3
.

The functor
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}
is well-defined in view of Lemma
5.4
.

Let
X
be in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
, and let

(C,\rho,\mu)
be an object in
{\mathbf{C}}^{G}(X)
.

Definition 5.6
.

We define the support of
(C,\rho,\mu)
by

{\mathtt{supp}}(C,\rho,\mu)\coloneqq\{x\in X\>|\>\mu(\{x\})\not=0\}\,.

Note that
{\mathtt{supp}}(C,\rho,\mu)
is a
G
-invariant subset of
X
.

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{Born}
, and let

(C,\rho,\mu)
be an object of
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{pt}}^{G}(X)
.

Definition 5.7
.

(C,\rho,\mu)
is locally
finite if:

1.

(C,\rho,\mu)
is small.

2.

{\mathtt{supp}}(C,\rho,\mu)
is a locally finite subset of
X
(see Definition
2.30
).

Definition 5.8
.

We let
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}(X)
denote the full subcategory of
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{pt}}^{G}(X)
of
locally finite objects.

Let
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
be a morphism in
G\mathbf{Born}
.
Recall Assumption
5.1
.

Lemma 5.9
.

The morphism
f_{*}\colon\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{pt}}^{G}(X)\to\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{pt}}^{G}(X^{\prime})
preserves
locally finite objects.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.4
the morphism
f_{*}
preserves small objects. By Lemma
4.14
it preserves the condition of being determined on points.

We now argue that for an object
(C,\rho,\mu)
in
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{pt}}^{G}(X)
we have

{\mathtt{supp}}f_{*}(C,\rho,\mu)\subseteq f({\mathtt{supp}}(C,\rho,\mu))\,.

Let
x^{\prime}
be a point in
{\mathtt{supp}}f_{*}(C,\rho,\mu)
. Then we have
(f_{*}\mu)(\{{x^{\prime}}\})=\mu(f^{-1}(\{x^{\prime}\}))\not=0
.
Since
(C,\rho,\mu)
is determined on points, there exists an
x
in
f^{-1}(\{x^{\prime}\})
such that
\mu(\{x\})\not=0
. Hence
x^{\prime}\in f({\mathtt{supp}}(C,\rho,\mu))
.

Since
f
is proper, it preserves locally finite subsets. Consequently,
{\mathtt{supp}}f_{*}(C,\rho,\mu)
is locally finite.
∎

Definition 5.10
.

We define the functor
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}\colon G\mathbf{Born}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
as the subfunctor of
{\mathbf{C}}^{G}_{\mathrm{pt}}\colon G\mathbf{Born}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
which sends
X
in
G\mathbf{Born}
to
the full subcategory
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}(X)
of locally finite objects in
{\mathbf{C}}^{G}_{\mathrm{pt}}(X)
defined in Definition
5.7
.

Note that the functor
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}
is well-defined in view of Lemma
5.9
.

Note that morphisms in
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
or
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}(X)
are just morphisms in
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}
.
In the following we define wide subcategories of these categories by putting additional conditions on the morphisms.
These conditions will depend on an additional coarse structure on
X
(see Definition
2.1
).

We have a forgetful functor
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to G\mathbf{Born}
which forgets the coarse structure. By precomposition with this forgetful functor we can consider

\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}
and
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}
as functors on
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
.
Employing the coarse structure we will construct subfunctors

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\quad\text{of}\quad G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\,.

(5.1)

and

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\quad\text{of}\quad G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\,.

(5.2)

Let
X
be a set,

U
be in
\mathcal{P}_{X\times X}
, and let
Y,Y^{\prime}
be in
\mathcal{P}_{X}
.

Definition 5.11
.

We say that
Y^{\prime}
is
U
-separated from
Y
if
U[Y]\cap Y^{\prime}=\emptyset
.

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{Born}
, let

(C,\rho,\mu)
and
(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be two objects in
\mathbf{C}^{G}(X)
, and let
A\colon(C,\rho,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be a morphism in
\mathbf{C}^{G}(X)
. Let
U
be in
\mathcal{P}_{X\times X}
.

Definition 5.12
.

We say that
A

is
U
-controlled if
for every two elements
Y,Y^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{P}_{X}
such that
Y^{\prime}
is
U
-separated from
Y

we have

\mu^{\prime}(Y^{\prime})A\mu(Y)=0\,.

(5.3)

Assume now that
X
is
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
with coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
, and let
A
be as above.

Definition 5.13
.

We say that
A
is controlled if there exists a
V
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
such that
A
is
V
-controlled.

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
.

Lemma 5.14
.

The controlled morphisms in
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
form a unital
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear
*
-category.

Proof.

We must show that the identities are controlled, and that the controlled morphisms are preserved by the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear structure, the composition and the involution.
The following assertions are straightforward to check.

If
(C,\rho,\mu)
is
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
, then
{\mathtt{id}}_{C}\colon(C,\rho,\mu)\to(C,\rho,\mu)
is
{\mathtt{diag}}(X)
-controlled.

If
A\colon(C,\rho,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
is
U
-controlled for some
U
on
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
, then
\lambda A
is
U
-controlled for all
\lambda
in
\mathbb{C}
.

If
Y
is
U^{-1}
-separated from
Y^{\prime}
, then
Y^{\prime}
is
U
-separated from
Y
. This implies that
A^{*}
is
U^{-1}
-controlled provided
A
was
U
-controlled.

For
i
in
\{0,1\}
let

A_{i}\colon(C,\rho,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be
U_{i}
-controlled morphisms for
U_{i}
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
.
Then
A_{0}+A_{1}
is
U_{0}\cup U_{1}
-controlled.

We now consider the composition.
Assume that
B\colon(C,\rho,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
is a
U
-controlled morphism and

A\colon(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\to(C^{\prime\prime},\rho^{\prime\prime},\mu^{\prime\prime})
is a
V
-controlled morphism in
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
.
Then we shall see that
A\circ B
is
V\circ U
-controlled. Assume that
Y^{\prime\prime}
is
V\circ U
-separated from
Y
.
Then
Y^{\prime\prime}
is
V
-separated from
U[Y]
and
X\setminus U[Y]
is
U
-separated from
Y
.
We now use the equalities
AB=A\mu^{\prime}(X)B
(since
\mu^{\prime}
is full) and
\mu^{\prime}(X)=\mu^{\prime}(U[Y])+\mu^{\prime}(X\setminus U[Y])
(by finite additivity of
\mu^{\prime}
) to get

\mu^{\prime\prime}(Y^{\prime\prime})AB\mu(Y)=\mu^{\prime\prime}(Y^{\prime\prime})A\mu^{\prime}(U[Y])B\mu(Y)+\mu^{\prime\prime}(Y^{\prime\prime})A\mu^{\prime}(X\setminus U[Y])B\mu(Y)=0

since
\mu^{\prime\prime}(Y^{\prime\prime})A\mu^{\prime}(U[Y])=0
and
\mu^{\prime}(X\setminus U[Y])B\mu(Y)=0
.

Since
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
contains
{\mathtt{diag}}(X)
and is closed under unions, compositions and inverses we see that
the controlled morphisms in
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
form a wide
unital
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear
*
-subcategory.
∎

Definition 5.15
.

We let
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
in
\mathbf{{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}}
be the wide
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear
*
-subcategory of controlled morphisms in
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
.

Note that
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
is in general not a
C^{*}
-category.

Example 5.16
.

If
X
has a maximal entourage, then
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
is a
C^{*}
-subcategory of
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
.
Indeed, if
U
is such a maximal entourage, then
it suffices to require that the morphisms are
U
-controlled.
This condition determines a closed subcategory.
∎

Let
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
be a morphism in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
.

Lemma 5.17
.

The morphism
f_{*}\colon\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)\to\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X^{\prime})
preserves controlled morphisms.

Proof.

Let
A\colon(C,\rho,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be a
V
-controlled morphism in
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
for
V
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
.
We claim that
f_{*}(A)=A\colon f_{*}(C,\rho,\mu)\to f_{*}(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
is
(f\times f)(V)
-controlled.

Let
Y,Y^{\prime}
be in
\mathcal{P}_{X^{\prime}}
and assume that

Y^{\prime}
is
(f\times f)(V)
-separated from
Y
.
Then
f^{-1}(Y^{\prime})
is
V
-separated from
f^{-1}(Y)
.
This follows from the obvious inclusion

V[f^{-1}(Y)]\subseteq f^{-1}(f\times f)(V)[Y]\,.

The claim follows now from

(f_{*}\mu^{\prime})(Y^{\prime})A(f_{*}\mu)(Y)=\mu^{\prime}(f^{-1}(Y^{\prime}))A(\mu(f^{-1}(Y))=0\,.\qed

Definition 5.18
.

We define the functor

\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to\mathbf{{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}}

as the subfunctor of
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
which sends
X
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
to
the wide
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear
*
-subcategory of controlled morphisms in
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
defined in Definition
5.15
.

Note that
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}
is well-defined in view of Lemma
5.17
.

Recall Assumption
5.1
.

Definition 5.19
.

We define the
functor

\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to\mathbf{{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}}\,,\quad X\mapsto\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)\cap\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}(X)\,,

(5.4)

where the intersection takes place in
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
.

Note that
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}
is a subfunctor of
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}
, and
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}
is a subfunctor of
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}
.
The inclusions
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)\to\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
or
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)\to\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}(X)
induce norms on the subcategories.
We can form the closures (on the level of morphism spaces) objectwise in order to obtain
C^{*}
-category-valued subfunctors.

Definition 5.20
.

1.

We define the functor

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}

as follows:

(a)

objects: For
X
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
we let
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)

be the closure of
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
with respect to the norm induced from
\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
.

(b)

morphisms: If
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
is any morphism in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
, then we let

f_{*}\colon\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)\to\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X^{\prime})
be the corresponding restriction of the morphism
f_{*}\colon\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)\to\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X^{\prime})
.

2.

We define the functor

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}

as follows:

(a)

objects: For
X
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
we let
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)

be the closure of
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
with respect to the norm induced from
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}(X)
.

(b)

morphisms: If
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
is any morphism in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
, then we let

f_{*}:\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)\to\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X^{\prime})
be the corresponding restriction of the morphism
f_{*}\colon\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}(X)\to\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G}(X^{\prime})
.

Remark 5.21
.

In view of the similarity of the definition of the categories
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
and
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
with the definition of Roe algebras
we call them Roe categories.

∎

This finishes the construction of the functors (
5.1
) and (
5.2
).

For fixed
X
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
the constructions of
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
and

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
are functorial in the datum
{\mathbf{C}}
