In order to get Diagram (
8.8
) must interchange the order of the colimits and the functor
-\rtimes G
in the middle column.
Since the structure maps of the systems are injective on objects this is again justified by
[
BELb
, Prop. 7.25]
.

It remains to show that the right square is excisive.
To this end we note that the right square is obtained from the square

\textstyle{\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{sm}}(({\mathcal{Y}}\cap Z)\subseteq Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{sm}}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ \mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{sm}}({\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)}

(8.9)

in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
by applying
-\rtimes G
.
The square (
8.9
) is excisive after forgetting the
G
-action by Lemma
6.10
. We finally use
[
Bun
, Thm. 8.14]
stating that
-\rtimes G
preserves excisive squares whose right corners are unital.
∎

Lemma 8.5
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}
admits countable AV-sums, then the functors
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}
and
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}
send flasque
G
-bornological coarse spaces to flasque
C^{*}
-categories.

Proof.

We give the argument in the case of
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}
. The case of
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}
is analoguous.
We argue as in the proof of Lemma
6.4
. Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
flasque with flasqueness implemented by
f\colon X\to X
. We construct the endofunctor

S\colon\mathbf{\bar{C}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)\to\mathbf{\bar{C}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{sm}}(X)
(not equivariant) as in the proof of Lemma
6.4
.

Using
[
BEa
, Prop. 11.4]
we extend the endofunctor
\bigoplus_{{\rm Nature}}{\mathtt{id}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}
to a weakly equivariant
functor
(\bigoplus_{{\rm Nature}}{\mathtt{id}}_{{\mathbf{C}}},\theta)
(see
[
Bun
, Def. 4.1]
). The same cocycle then provides an extension

(S,\theta)
of
S
to a weakly equivariant endofunctor.
By
[
Bun
, Prop. 7.12]
we get an endofunctor

(S,\theta)\rtimes G\colon\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}(X)\to\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}(X)\,.

The Equality (
6.2
) induces an equality (adopting appropriate choices for the sum)

{\mathtt{id}}_{\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}(X)}\oplus\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}(f)\circ(S\rtimes G)=S\rtimes G\,.

(8.10)

By the Lemma
8.2
and since
f
is close to the identity we have a unitary isomorphism
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}(f)\circ(S\rtimes G)\simeq S\rtimes G
. Consequently, we have a unitary isomorphism

{\mathtt{id}}_{\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}(X)}\oplus(S\rtimes G)\cong S\rtimes G

showing that

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
is flasque.
∎

Let
\mathrm{Hg}\colon C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathbf{M}
be a functor.

Definition 8.6
.

We define the functors

\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Hg}}\mathbf{M}

and

\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c,G}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Hg}}\mathbf{M}\,.

The subscript
G
should indicate that the functors involve taking
G
-coinvariants which is here explicitly realized in terms of the crossed product.
The subscript
c
indicates that
the construction involves the continuous functor
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}
. But note that the functors introduced in Definition
8.6
are not continuous except in degenerate cases (see Example
8.10
).

Recall Definition
3.23
of a finitary homological functor and Definition
2.37
of an equivariant coarse homology theory.

Theorem 8.7
.

Assume:

1.

\mathrm{Hg}
is a finitary homological functor.

2.

{\mathbf{C}}
admits countable AV-sums and is effectively additive (and
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
is idempotent complete
in the case of
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}
).

Then

\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}
and
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c,G}
are equivariant coarse homology theories which in addition send weak coarse equivalences to equivalences.

Proof.

We give the proof for
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}
. The case of
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c,G}
is analoguous.
Note that
\mathbf{M}
is stable since it is the target of a homological functor.
We now verify the conditions listed in Definition
2.37
.

1.

\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}
is coarsely invariant: Indeed, by Lemma
8.2
the functor
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}
sends weak coarse equivalences to unitary equivalences. Further,
\mathrm{Hg}
sends unitary equivalences to equivalences. So
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}
sends (even weak) coarse equivalences to equivalences.

2.

\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}
is
u
-continuous: This follows from Lemma
8.3
stating that
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}
is
u
-continuous and the fact that
\mathrm{Hg}
preserves filtered colimits, since we assume that it is finitary.

3.

\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}
is excisive: We clearly have
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}(\emptyset)\simeq 0_{\mathbf{M}}
.
Let now
X
be in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
, and let
({\mathcal{Y}},Z)
be an invariant complementary pair on
X
.
We apply
\mathrm{Hg}
to the Diagram (
8.8
) and get

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hg}\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{Y}}\cap Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hg}(\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{sm}}(({\mathcal{Y}}\cap Z)\subseteq Z)\rtimes G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hg}\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hg}\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{Y}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hg}(\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{sm}}({\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq X)\rtimes G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hg}\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}(X)}

Since
\mathrm{Hg}
preserves filtered colimits and sends unitary equivalences to equivalences,
the left horizontal maps are filtered colimits of equivalences and hence equivalences.
The right square is the image under the homological functor
\mathrm{Hg}
of an excisive square and hence a cocartesian square. Consequently, the outer square is cocartesian.

4.

\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}
annihilates flasques: Indeed, if
X
is flasque, then
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{sm},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
is flasque by Lemma

8.5
. Then
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}(X)\simeq 0
by
[
BEa
, Prop. 13.13]
.∎

Assumption 8.8
.

From now it will be a standing hypothesis that
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
is effectively additive
and admits countable AV-sums, when we talk about the coarse homology theories

\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c}^{G}
and
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c,G}
.

When we consider

\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}
or
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}
, then
we will assume that
{\mathbf{C}}
admits countable AV-sums and that
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
is idempotent complete.

Additional assumptions will be stated if necessary.

∎

Remark 8.9
.

Note that the equivariant coarse homology theories from Theorem
8.7
send all weak coarse equivalences to equivalences. In contrast, by
Proposition
7.9
the functor

\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c}
sends weak coarse equivalences to equivalences only under additional assumptions.

∎

Example 8.10
.

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
. We assume that there exists a maximal entourage
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
, and that all coarse components of
X
(Definition
10.4
) are bounded, i.e., the equivalence classes for the equivalence relation
U
belong to
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
. Then
the canonical projection
X\to\pi_{0}(X)_{min,min}
is a weak coarse equivalence, where
\pi_{0}(X)
denotes the
G
-set of coarse components of
X
.
In this case

\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c,G}(X)\to\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c,G}(\pi_{0}(X)_{min,min})

is an equivalence.

A concrete example of such a space is
G_{max,max}
.
We have
\pi_{0}(G_{max,max})\cong*
.
Hence
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c,G}(G_{max,max})\to\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c,G}(*)
is an equivalence. Note that the target of this morphism is given by
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c,G}(*)\cong\mathrm{Hg}({\mathbf{C}}^{u}\rtimes G)
, which is in general non-trivial. If
G
is infinite, then
G_{max,max}
does not have any invariant, locally finite subsets.
This shows that the coarse homology theory
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c,G}
is in general not continuous. See also Example
7.10
.

∎

Recall the Definition
2.40
of a strong equivariant coarse homology theory.

Proposition 8.11
.

The coarse homology theories
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}
and
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c,G}
are strong.

Proof.

We give the argument in the case of
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}
. The case of
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{c,G}
is analoguous.
Let
X
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
be weakly flasque (Definition
2.39
) with weak flasqueness implemented by
f\colon X\to X
. We apply
\mathrm{Hg}
to the Equation (
8.10
)
obtained in the proof of Lemma
8.5
.
Using the additivity of
\mathrm{Hg}
shown in
[
BEa
, Prop. 13.11]
we get

{\mathtt{id}}_{\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}(X)}+\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}(X)(f)\circ\mathrm{Hg}(S\rtimes G)=\mathrm{Hg}(S\rtimes G)

in
{\mathtt{End}}_{\mathbf{M}}(\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}(X))
.
From Condition
2.39
.
1

we get

\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}(X)(f)\simeq{\mathtt{id}}_{\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}(X)}
, and the resulting relation

{\mathtt{id}}_{\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}(X)}+\mathrm{Hg}(S\rtimes G)=\mathrm{Hg}(S\rtimes G)
implies
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{G}(X)\simeq 0
.
∎

9
Calculations

In this section we calculate the values of some of the functors introduced in the preceding sections on bornological coarse spaces derived from
G
-sets.
The calculations for
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c}
are of particular relevance
for the results stated in detail in Section
13
.

We fix
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
and assume that it is effectively additive. Applying the functor from (
8.4
) to
X
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
we obtain the object
\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})
. Instead of the reduced crossed product as in Definition
8.1
we can form, as a start, the algebraic crossed product
\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G
in
\mathbf{{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}}
, see
[
Bun
, Defn. 5.1]
.
On the other hand we have the uncompleted Roe category
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,min})
in
\mathbf{{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}}
, introduced in Definition
5.19
, where
G_{can,min}
is defined in Example
2.13
and the symmetric monoidal structure
\otimes
on
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
is explained in Definition
2.15
.

Proposition 9.1
.

Assume:

1.

Every locally finite subset of
X
has a maximal entourage.

2.

{\mathbf{C}}
admits orthogonal AV-sums of cardinality
|G|
.

Then
there exists a unitary equivalence

\phi\colon\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G\to\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,min})

in
\mathbf{{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}}
which is natural in
X
.

Proof.

By Assumption
2
for every object
C
in
{\mathbf{C}}
we can fix an orthogonal AV-sum
(\bigoplus_{g\in G}gC,(e^{C}_{g})_{g\in G})
.
For any
X
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
satisfying Condition
1
we will then construct a morphism

\phi\colon\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G\to\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,min})

in
\mathbf{{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}}
which turns out to be natural in
X
.
To show that it is a unitary equivalence we will furthermore construct a morphism

\psi\colon\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,min})\to\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G

in
\mathbf{{}^{*}{\mathbf{Cat}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}}
(with no naturality requirements)
and unitary isomorphisms

u\colon\psi\circ\phi\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathtt{id}}_{\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G}\,,\quad v\colon\phi\circ\psi\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathtt{id}}_{\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,min})}\,.

Condition
1
implies that every morphism in
\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)
is controlled.
Indeed, if
(C,\mu)
and
(C^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
are objects of
\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)
, then by Definition
5.7
.
2

L\coloneqq{\mathtt{supp}}(C,\mu)\cup{\mathtt{supp}}(C^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})

is a locally finite subset of
X
. By Condition
1
we can find an entourage
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
such that
U\cap(L\times L)
is maximal in the coarse structure on
L
induced from
X
. Then every morphism
(C,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
in
\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)
is
U
-controlled (see Example
5.16
).

Note that for a general
G
-bornological space
Y
the morphisms
between objects in
\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(Y)
are limits of sequences of controlled morphisms, but in general not controlled themselves. The fact that the morphisms are themselves controlled in our present situation
will be used below in order to see that the morphism defined by (
9.1
) is controlled.

In order to construct
\phi
, by
[
Bun
, Lem. 5.7]

it suffices to construct a covariant representation
(\sigma,\pi)
of
\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)
on
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,min})
.
We construct the morphism
\sigma:\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)\to\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,min})
as follows:

1.

objects:
\sigma
sends the object
(C,\mu)
in
\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)
to

\sigma(C,\mu):=\big(\bigoplus_{g\in G}gC,\rho,\tilde{\mu}\big)

in

\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,min})
. The
first entry uses the choice of the orthogonal AV-sums adopted at the beginning of this proof. The projection-valued measure
\tilde{\mu}
is determined by

\tilde{\mu}(\{(x,g)\})\coloneqq e^{C}_{g}g\mu(g^{-1}(\{x\}))e_{g}^{C,*}

for all
(x,g)
in
X\times G
. Finally, the unitary cocycle
\rho\coloneqq(\rho_{h})_{h\in G}
is defined by

\rho_{h}\coloneqq\sum_{g\in G}he^{C}_{g}e_{hg}^{C,*}\colon\bigoplus_{g\in G}gC\to h\bigoplus_{g\in G}gC\ .

(9.1)

In order to show that
\rho
and
\tilde{\mu}
exist as morphisms in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
we use
[
BEa
, Lem. 7.8]
.
One checks that
\rho
satisfies the cocycle conditions, and that
\tilde{\mu}
is equivariant (see Definitions
4.1
.
1
and
4.3
.
3
).
One furthermore checks that
\phi(C,\mu)
is locally finite.

2.

morphisms:
\sigma
sends
A\colon(C,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
in
\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)
to

\sigma(A)\coloneqq\sum_{h\in G}e^{C^{\prime}}_{h}hAe_{h}^{C,*}\,.

(9.2)

where we
use
[
BEa
, Lem. 7.8]

in order to see that this formula defines a multiplier morphism.
One checks in a straightforward manner that

\sigma(A)
is equivariant (see Definition
4.1
.
2
).
In order to check that
\sigma(A)
is controlled observe that
if

A
is
U
-controlled for
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
, then
\sigma(A)
is
G(U\times{\mathtt{diag}}(G))
-controlled, and that
G(U\times{\mathtt{diag}}(G))
is a coarse entourage of
X\otimes G_{can,min}
.

We next construct the family

\pi=(\pi(h))_{h\in G}
of natural transformations

\pi(h):\sigma\to h^{*}\sigma
by setting

\pi(h)_{(C,\mu)}:=\sum_{g\in G}e^{hC}_{g}e^{C,*}_{gh}:\bigoplus_{g\in G}gC\to\bigoplus_{g}ghC

(9.3)

for every
(C,\mu)
in
\mathrm{Ob}(\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X))
.
We again use
[
BEa
, Lem. 7.8]

in order to see that this formula defines a unitary multiplier morphism. As a morphism

\big(\bigoplus_{g\in G}gC,\rho,\tilde{\mu}\big)\to\big(\bigoplus_{g\in G}ghC,\rho,\tilde{\mu}\big)

in
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,min})

the morphism
\pi(h)_{(C,\mu)}
is

G({\mathtt{diag}}(X)\times\{(e,h^{-1})\})
-controlled, and
G({\mathtt{diag}}(X)\times\{(e,h^{-1})\})
is a coarse entourage of
X\otimes G_{can,min}
.
One checks that

(\pi(h)_{(C,\mu)})_{(C,\mu)\in\mathrm{Ob}(\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X))}
is a natural transformation from
\sigma
to
h^{*}\sigma
, and that the family

(\phi(h))_{h\in G}
satisfies the cocycle condition
\ell^{*}\pi(h)\pi(\ell)=\pi(h\ell)
for all
h,\ell
in
G
.

We can now read off the formulas for
\phi
:

1.

objects:

\phi(C,\mu)\coloneqq\big(\bigoplus_{g\in G}gC,\rho,\tilde{\mu}\big)\,.

2.

morphisms: We will use the pair notation for morphisms as in
[
Bun
, Defn. 5.1]
.

\phi(A,g)\coloneqq\sum_{h\in G}e^{C^{\prime}}_{hg^{-1}}hAe_{h}^{C,*}\,.

(9.4)

By an inspection one checks that
\phi
is is natural
X
.

Next we construct
\psi
.

1.

objects: Let
(C,\rho,\tilde{\mu})
be an object of
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,min})
. Since
{\mathbf{C}}
is effectively additive by assumption
and
(C,\rho,\tilde{\mu})
is locally finite we can
choose an image
(\tilde{C},\tilde{u})
in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
of the projection
{\tilde{\mu}}(X\times\{e\})
. Then we define
\mu\colon\mathcal{P}_{X}\to\mathrm{Proj}(\tilde{C})
to be the restriction of
{\tilde{u}}^{*}{\tilde{\mu}}{\tilde{u}}
to

X\times\{e\}
. We now set

\psi(C,\rho,\tilde{\mu})\coloneqq(\tilde{C},\mu)\,.

(9.5)

One checks that
(\tilde{C},\mu)
is locally finite.

2.

morphisms: Let
A\colon(C,\rho,\tilde{\mu})\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},{\tilde{\mu}}^{\prime})
be a morphism in
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,min})
.
For every
g
in
G
we set

A_{g}\coloneqq(g^{-1}{\tilde{u}}^{\prime})^{*}\rho^{\prime}_{g^{-1}}A{\tilde{u}}\colon\tilde{C}\to g^{-1}\tilde{C}^{\prime}\,.

(9.6)

If
A
is
U
-controlled for
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X\otimes G_{can,min}}
, then
A_{g}
is
U\cap((X\times X)\times{(e,e)})
-controlled for all
g
in
G
. Furthermore, if
A_{g}\not=0
, then
g^{-1}\in\mathrm{pr}_{G\times G}(U)[\{e\}]
.
In view of the definition of the coarse structure on
X\otimes G_{can,min}
we have
\mathrm{pr}_{G\times G}(U)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{G_{can,min}}
and therefore
\mathrm{pr}_{G\times G}(U)[\{e\}]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{G_{can,min}}
, i.e., this set is finite.
We now define

\psi(A)\coloneqq\sum_{g\in G}(A_{g},g)\,.

(9.7)

We just have seen that this sum has only finitely many non-trivial terms and therefore defines a morphism from
(\tilde{C},\mu)
to
(\tilde{C}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
in

\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G
. One checks that this construction is compatible with the composition and the involution.

We now construct the unitary isomorphisms
u
and
v
.

1.

u\colon\psi\circ\phi\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathtt{id}}
:
Let
(C,\mu)
be an object in
\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X)
. Then
\phi(C,\mu)=(\bigoplus_{g\in G}gC,{\rho,\tilde{\mu}})
.
In the construction of
\psi
we have chosen the image
(\widetilde{\bigoplus_{g\in G}gC},{\tilde{u}})
of
\tilde{\mu}(X\times\{e\})

such that

u_{(C,\mu)}\coloneqq e^{C,*}_{e}{\tilde{u}}\colon\widetilde{\bigoplus_{g\in G}gC}\to C

is a unitary multiplier isomorphism. The family
u\coloneqq(u_{(C,\mu)})_{(C,\mu)\in\mathrm{Ob}(\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X))}
provides the desired unitary isomorphism.

2.

v\colon\phi\circ\psi\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathtt{id}}
: Let
(C,\rho,\tilde{\mu})
be an object of
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,min})
.
In the construction of
\psi
we have fixed an image
(\tilde{C},\tilde{u})
of
{\tilde{\mu}}(X\times\{e\})
such that
\psi(C,\rho,\tilde{\mu})=(\tilde{C},\mu)
, see (
9.5
).
Then
\phi(\tilde{C},\mu)=(\bigoplus_{g\in G}g\tilde{C},\rho,\tilde{\tilde{\mu}})
.

Using the invariance condition for
\tilde{\mu}
stated in Definition
4.3
.
3
, one checks that
(g\tilde{C},\rho_{g}^{-1}g(u))
represents an image of
{\tilde{\mu}}(X\times\{g\})
. Since
(C,\rho,\mu)
is determined on points, by Lemma
4.13

the object
C
is the orthogonal AV-sum of the images of the family of projections
\tilde{\mu}(X\times\{g\})
. Therefore, setting

u_{g}\coloneqq\rho_{g}^{-1}g(u)\,,

(9.8)

using
[
BEa
, Lem. 7.8]
we get a unitary multiplier isomorphism

v_{(C,\rho,\mu)}\colon\bigoplus_{g\in G}g\tilde{C}\xrightarrow{\sum_{g\in G}u_{g}e^{\tilde{C},*}_{g}}C\,.

(9.9)

The family

(v_{(C,\rho,\mu)})_{(C,\rho,\mu)\in\mathrm{Ob}(\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,min}))}

is the desired unitary isomorphism
v
.∎

We consider
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
and its full subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{u}
of unital objects in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})
. Under the condition that
{\mathbf{C}}^{u}
is (finitely) additive, in

[
BEa
, Constr. 19.6]
and
[
BEa
, Def. 19.9]
we introduced the functors

{\mathbf{C}}^{u}[-]\colon G{\mathbf{Set}}\to{{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})}\,,\quad{\mathbf{C}}^{u}[-]\rtimes_{r}G\colon G{\mathbf{Set}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\,.

(9.10)

In Assertion
3
of the following proposition we use the reduced crossed product from
[
BEa
, Thm. 12.1]
.

Proposition 9.2
.

We assume that
{\mathbf{C}}
is effectively additive and
{\mathbf{C}}^{u}
is additive
3
3

3

For the first two statements we could drop the essential additivity assumption.
.
For
X
in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
we have the following natural isomorphisms (or unitary equivalence, respectively):

1.

\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X_{min,max})\cong{\mathbf{C}}^{u}[X]
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})
.

2.

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}(X_{min,max})\cong{\mathbf{C}}^{u}[X]\rtimes G
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
.

3.

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X_{min,max}\otimes G_{can,min})\simeq{\mathbf{C}}^{u}[X]\rtimes_{r}G
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
(under the additional assumption that

{\mathbf{C}}
admits orthogonal AV-sums of cardinality
|G|
)

Proof.

This isomorphism in Assertion
1
follows from a comparison of the construction
[
BEa
, Constr. 19.6]
of
{\mathbf{C}}^{u}[X]
with the definition of

\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X_{min,max})
, as we explain in the following.
Recall that an object of
\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X_{min,max})
is a pair
(C,\mu)
of an object
C
of
{\mathbf{C}}

and a function
\mu\colon\mathcal{P}_{X}\to\mathrm{Proj}(C)
.
Since
(C,\mu)
is locally finite the projection
\mu(\{x\})
belongs to
{\mathbf{C}}
for every
x
in
X
. Furthermore, since
X_{min,max}
has the maximal bornology, the set
{\mathtt{supp}}(C,\mu)=\{x\in X\mid\mu(\{x\})\not=0\}
is finite so that
\sum_{x\in X}\mu(\{x\})={\mathtt{id}}_{C}
is a morphism in
{\mathbf{C}}
. In particular,
C
belongs to
{\mathbf{C}}^{u}
. On objects the asserted isomorphism sends
(C,\mu)
to
(C,(\mu(\{x\}))_{x\in X})
of
{\mathbf{C}}^{u}[\tilde{X}]
.

Since
X_{min,max}
has the minimal coarse structure the condition that a morphism
A\colon(C,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
is controlled corresponds to the condition
\mu^{\prime}(\{x^{\prime}\})A\mu(\{x\})=0
for all
x,x^{\prime}
in
X
with
x\not=x
as required for a morphism in
{\mathbf{C}}^{u}[X]
.
The asserted isomorphism identifies
A\colon(C,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
as above with the same morphism now considered as a morphism in
A:(C,(\mu(\{x\}))_{x\in X})\to(C^{\prime},(\mu^{\prime}(\{x\}))_{x\in X})
.

Assertion
2
immediately follows from
Assertion
1
and the Definition
8.1
.
2
.

In order to show Assertion
3
note that we have a faithful morphism
\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X_{min,max})\to{\mathbf{C}}

which sends
(C,\mu)
to
C
and is the obvious inclusion on the level of morphisms. Since
-\rtimes_{r}G
preserves faithful (or equivalently, isometric) morphisms by
[
BEa
, Thm. 12.1]

we can define the reduced norm on
\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X_{min,max})\rtimes^{\mathrm{alg}}G
using the induced covariant representation
of
\mathbf{\tilde{\bar{C}}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}_{\mathrm{lf}}(X_{min,max})
on
\mathbf{L}^{2}(G,{\mathbf{W}}^{\mathrm{nu}}{\mathbf{C}})
(see
[
BEa
, Defs. 2.35 & 12.2]
).
By an inspection one observes that the formulas
[
BEa
, (12.4)]
and
[
BEa
, (12.5)]
for this covariant representation coincide with the formulas
(
9.2
) and (
9.3
).
We conclude that the norm induced from
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,min})
through the unitary equivalence
\phi
from Proposition
9.1
is the norm of the reduced crossed product.
Therefore the unitary equivalence
\phi
extends by continuity to the unitary equivalence as asserted in
Assertion
3
.
∎

Remark 9.3
.

In general the maximal and the reduced crossed products do not coincide. We have a canonical morphism from the maximal crossed product to the reduced one. Combining this with Proposition
9.2
.(
2
&
3
)
we get a functor

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf},G}^{\mathrm{ctr}}(X_{min,max})\to\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X_{min,max}\otimes G_{can,min})

(9.11)

which in general is not an equivalence.
This stays in contrast to

[
BCKW
, Cor. 5.4]
which asserts that the analogue of this morphism in the case of controlled objects in left-exact
\infty
-categories
is always an equivalence.

If
G
is amenable, then as a consequence of
[
BEa
, Thm. 12.27]
the functor in (
9.11
) is an isomorphism.

∎

10
Transfers

In
[
BEKW20a
, Defn. 2.27]
we introduced an extension

\iota\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to G\mathbf{BornCoarse}_{{\mathtt{tr}}}

(10.1)

of the category of
G
-bornological coarse spaces to an
\infty
-category
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}_{{\mathtt{tr}}}
.
It has the same objects as
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
. Its
1
-morphisms are spans

\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y}

where
f
is a morphism in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
which is in addition bornological, see Definition
2.7
(
2
), and where
w
is a bounded covering (see Definition
10.5
below).
The higher morphisms are diagrams which exhibit compositions of spans. We refer to
[
BEKW20a
, Defn. 2.27]
for more details.
They will not be needed in the present paper.

We consider a functor
E\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to\mathbf{M}
.

Definition 10.1
.

E
admits transfers if there exists a functor
E_{{\mathtt{tr}}}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}_{{\mathtt{tr}}}\to\mathbf{M}
and an equivalence

E_{{\mathtt{tr}}}\circ\iota\simeq E
.

In the following it is useful to model
\infty
-categories by quasi-categories, and to distinguish the notation for categories
{\mathcal{C}}
from the notation
{\mathtt{N}}({\mathcal{C}})
for the corresponding quasi-categories. Basic references for this model are
[
Lur09
,
Lur
]
.

Recall that the category
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
is the underlying
1
-category of a strict
(2,1)
-category
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{2,1}
whose
2
-isomorphisms are unitary isomorphisms of functors. We have a
2
-nerve functor
{\mathtt{N}}_{2}
from
(2,1)
-categories to quasi-categories. This functor sends a
(2,1)
-category to the coherent nerve of the fibrant simplicial category obtained by applying the usual nerve functor to the morphism groupoids.
We have a canonical embedding

\ell_{2}\colon{\mathtt{N}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})\to{\mathtt{N}}_{2}(C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{2,1})\,.

Assume that
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})

admits all very small AV-sums and is therefore in particular effectively additive.
We then have the functor

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}

defined in Definition
5.20
.
2
.
We can consider the functor

{\mathtt{N}}(G\mathbf{BornCoarse})\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{N}}(\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}})}{\mathtt{N}}(C^{*}\mathbf{Cat})\xrightarrow{\ell_{2}}{\mathtt{N}}_{2}(C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{2,1})\,.

We want to assert that this functor admits transfers.
The more precise formulation of Definition
10.1
requires that there is
a functor

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf},\mathrm{tr}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}_{{\mathtt{tr}}}\to{\mathtt{N}}_{2}(C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{2,1})
such that

\ell_{2}\circ{\mathtt{N}}(\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}})\simeq\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf},\mathrm{tr}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}\circ\iota\,.

(10.2)

The following is the main theorem of the present section.

Theorem 10.2
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}
admits all very small AV-sums, then
the functor

\ell_{2}\circ{\mathtt{N}}(\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}})\colon{\mathtt{N}}(G\mathbf{BornCoarse})\to{\mathtt{N}}_{2}(C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{2,1})

admits transfers.

Remark 10.3
.

In the proof of Theorem
10.2
we need the existence of AV-sums in
{\mathbf{C}}
for arbitrary very small sets. This is stronger then the requirements for the existence of the coarse homology theory
\mathrm{Hg}{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c}
which are effective additivity and countable AV-sums.

We can not expect that the functor
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}
with values in the ordinary category
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
admits transfers. The problematic point is that the contravariant extension of the functor
to bounded coverings involves choices of orthogonal AV-sums on the level of objects.
As a consequence, functoriality is satisfied only up to canonical
2
-isomorphisms.

∎

Before we start with the proof of Theorem
10.2

we recall some further notions from coarse geometry which will be used in the arguments.

Let
W
be in
G\mathbf{Coarse}
. Then we consider the element

U_{\pi_{0}(W)}\coloneqq\bigcup_{U\in{\mathcal{C}}_{W}}U

of
\mathcal{P}_{W\times W}
.
Using the conditions on
{\mathcal{C}}_{W}
listed in Definition
2.1
we see that
U_{\pi_{0}(W)}
is a
G
-invariant equivalence relation on
W
.

Definition 10.4
.

The equivalence classes in
W
for
U_{\pi_{0}(W)}
are the coarse components of
W
.

We let
\pi_{0}(W)
denote the set of coarse components. Since
U_{\pi_{0}(W)}
is
G
-invariant, the group
G
acts on the set

\pi_{0}(W)
.

Let
W,X
be objects of
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
and
w\colon W\to X
be a morphism between the underlying
G
-coarse spaces.

Definition 10.5
.

w
is a bounded covering if:

1.

For every coarse component
V
of
W
the restriction
f_{|V}\colon V\to f(V)
is an isomorphism of coarse spaces from
V
to a coarse component of
X
.

2.

The coarse structure of
W
is generated by the entourages
U_{\pi_{0}(W)}\cap(w\times w)^{-1}(U)
for all
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
.

3.

w
is bornological.

4.

For every
B
in
{\mathcal{B}}_{W}
there exists a finite bound (depending on
B
) on the cardinality of the fibres of
\pi_{0}(B)\to\pi_{0}(X)
.

We will indicate bounded coverings using the double-headed arrow symbol
.

We consider objects
W,V,U,Z
in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
and a square

\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{w}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{Z}

in
G\mathbf{Coarse}
.

Definition 10.6

(
[
BEKW20a
, Def. 2.19]
)
.

The square is admissible if it has the following properties:

1.

The square is cartesian in
G\mathbf{Coarse}
.

2.

f
and
g
are proper and bornological.

3.

u
and
w
are bounded coverings.

Proof of Theorem
10.2
.

We must construct a functor

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf},\mathrm{tr}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}_{{\mathtt{tr}}}\to{\mathtt{N}}_{2}(C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}_{2,1})

satisfying (
10.2
).
We can use the method explained
in
[
BEKW20a
, Sec. 2.1]
. Following this reference, in order to construct
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf},\mathrm{tr}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}
we must provide the following data:

1.

We start with the functor
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
.

2.

For every bounded covering
w\colon W\to Z
we must provide a
1
-morphism

w^{*}:\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(Z)\to\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(W)\,.

3.

For every two composable bounded coverings
w\colon W\to Z
and
v\colon V\to W
we must provide a
2
-isomorphism

a_{v,w}\colon(w\circ v)^{*}\Rightarrow v^{*}\circ w^{*}\,.

4.

For every admissible square

\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{w}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{Z}

we must provide a
2
-isomorphism

b_{g,u}\colon f_{*}\circ w^{*}\Rightarrow u^{*}\circ g_{*}\,.

This data must satisfy the following conditions:

1.

If the bounded covering
w\colon W\to Z
is an isomorphism, then
w^{*}=w^{-1}_{*}
.

2.

If the composable bounded coverings
w\colon W\to Z
and
v\colon V\to W
are both isomorphisms, then

a_{v,w}={\mathtt{id}}
. This equality makes sense, because in this case
v^{*}w^{*}=(wv)^{*}
by the strict functoriality of
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}
.

3.

If
w\colon W\to Z
and
v\colon V\to W
and
u\colon U\to V
are three composable bounded coverings, then
the square of
2
-isomorphisms

\textstyle{(w\circ v\circ u)^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{vu,w}}
\scriptstyle{a_{u,wv}}
\textstyle{(v\circ u)^{*}\circ w^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{u,v}\circ w^{*}}
\textstyle{u^{*}\circ(w\circ v)^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{*}\circ a_{v,w}}
\textstyle{u^{*}\circ v^{*}\circ w^{*}}

commutes.

4.

In the case of an admissible square with morphisms
w,f,g,u
, if
u
(and therefore
w
) is an isomorphism,
then we have
b_{g,u}={\mathtt{id}}
. This equality again makes sense since

f_{*}w^{*}=f_{*}w^{-1}_{*}=u^{-1}_{*}g_{*}=u^{*}g_{*}

by the strict functoriality of
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}
.

5.

In the case of an admissible square with morphisms
w,f,g,u
, if
f
and
g
are identities and therefore
w=u
, then we have
b_{g,u}={\mathtt{id}}
.

6.

For every diagram

\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{n}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{Z}

consisting of two admissible squares we have the relation

b_{gh,r}=(b_{g,r}\circ h_{*})(n_{*}\circ b_{h,s})\,.

(10.3)

7.

For every diagram

\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{Y}

consisting of two admissible squares we have the relation

(a_{s,v}\circ f_{*})b_{f,vs}=(s^{*}\circ b_{f,v})(b_{h,s}\circ u^{*})(m_{*}\circ a_{t,u})\,.

(10.4)

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{BornCoarse}
, and let
(C,\rho,\mu)
be an object of
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
. For a subset
Y
of
X

we will use the notation
(C(Y),u^{C}_{Y})
in order to denote a representative of the image in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
of the projection
\mu(Y)
.
Our language will be adapted to
{\mathbf{C}}
so that in the following morphisms in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
will be called multiplier morphisms.

We let
(\bigoplus_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}C(Y),(e_{Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)})
denote a representative of the orthogonal AV-sum in
{\mathbf{C}}
of the family
(C(Y))_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
.
By Lemma
4.13
applied to the partition
(Y)_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
of
X

we have a unitary multiplier isomorphism

\sum_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}u^{C}_{Y}e_{Y}^{*}\colon\bigoplus_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}C(Y)\xrightarrow{\cong}C\,.

Let
(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be a second object,
(\bigoplus_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}C^{\prime}(Y),(e^{\prime}_{Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)})
be the corresponding choice of an orthogonal AV-sum, and let
A\colon(C,\rho,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be a morphism in
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
.
For
Y,Y^{\prime}
in
\pi_{0}(X)
we define the multiplier morphism

A_{Y^{\prime},Y}\coloneqq u_{Y^{\prime}}^{C^{\prime},*}Au^{C}_{Y}\colon C(Y)\to C^{\prime}(Y^{\prime})\,.

Since
A
is
U_{\pi_{0}}(X)
-controlled we actually have
A_{Y^{\prime},Y}=0
if
Y\not=Y^{\prime}
.
In order to simplify the notation we set

A_{Y}\coloneqq A_{Y,Y}\,.

The morphism
A
is uniquely determined by the family
(A_{Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
.
In the following we discuss how one can reconstruct the morphism
A
from a family
(A_{Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
.
Assume that a family of multiplier morphisms
(A_{Y}\colon C(Y)\to C^{\prime}(Y))_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
is given.
We say that
(A_{Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
is bounded if

\sup_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}\|A_{Y}\|<\infty
.
If
(A_{Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
is bounded, then using
[
BEa
, Lem. 7.8]
we can define a multiplier morphism
A\colon C\to C^{\prime}
uniquely such that

\textstyle{\bigoplus_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}C(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\oplus_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}A_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\sum_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}u^{C}_{Y}e^{*}_{Y}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}C^{\prime}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\sum_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}u^{C^{\prime}}_{Y}e^{\prime,*}_{Y}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}}

commutes.

By Definition
4.3
.
3
we have the relation
\rho_{g}\circ\mu(Y)=g\mu(g^{-1}Y)\circ\rho_{g}
. We therefore get a canonical unitary multiplier

\rho_{g,Y}\coloneqq(gu^{C}_{g^{-1}Y})^{*}\circ\rho_{g}\circ u^{C}_{Y}\colon C(Y)\to g(C(g^{-1}Y))\,.

(10.5)

We say that the family
(A_{Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
is equivariant if it satisfies

gA_{g^{-1}Y}\circ\rho_{g,Y}=\rho^{\prime}_{g,Y}\circ A_{Y}

for all
g
in
G
. If
(A_{Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
is bounded and equivariant, then it induces a morphism of
G
-objects
A\colon(C,\rho)\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
in
{(\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}})}^{G}
, see Definition
4.1
.

Finally one must ensure that
A
is controlled. We consider the projection-valued function

\mu_{Y}\coloneqq u^{C,*}_{Y}\mu_{|\mathcal{P}_{Y}}u^{C}_{Y}\colon\mathcal{P}_{Y}\to\mathrm{Proj}(C(Y))\,.

(10.6)

Then

(C(Y),\mu_{Y})
is an object of
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{\mathrm{ctr}}(Y)
.
Assume that
(A_{Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
is an equivariant and bounded family.
Let
U
be in
\mathcal{P}_{X\times X}
. We say that
(A_{Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
is
U
-controlled if
A_{Y}
is
U\cap(Y\times Y)
-controlled for all
Y
in
\pi_{0}(X)
.
If
(A_{Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
is
U
-controlled, then the induced morphism
A\colon(C,\rho,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
is a
U
-controlled morphism in
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
.

We again consider an equivariant and bounded family
(A_{Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
.
We now assume that there exists a sequence
((A_{n,Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}

of bounded equivariant families and a family
(U_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{X}
such that
(A_{n,Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
is

U_{n}
-controlled for every

n
in
{\rm Nature}
, and

\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\to\infty}\sup_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}\|A_{n,Y}-A_{Y}\|=0\,.

Let
A_{n}\colon(C,\rho,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be the
U_{n}
-controlled morphism in
\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
defined by the family
(A_{n,Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
.
Then we have
\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\to\infty}A_{n}=A
in
{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{C}}}(C,C^{\prime})
, and consequently
A\colon(C,\rho,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
is a morphism in
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
.

Note that if
A\colon(C,\rho,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
is a morphism in
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)
, then the associated family

(A_{Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(X)}
has all the properties needed for the reconstruction of
A
.

We now start with the construction of the data for
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf},\mathrm{tr}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}
.

1.

The functor
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}\colon G\mathbf{BornCoarse}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}
is already constructed, see Definition
5.20
.
2
.

2.

Let
w\colon W\to Z
be a bounded covering. We construct the functor

w^{*}\colon\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(Z)\to\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(W)\,.

(a)

objects: Let
(C,\rho,\mu)
be an object in
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(Z)
. We choose an orthonormal AV-sum
(\bigoplus_{Y\in\pi_{0}(W)}C(w(Y)),(e^{w^{*}}_{Y})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(W)})
in
{\mathbf{C}}
.
In particular, if
w
is an isomorphism, then we choose the representative
(C,(u^{C}_{w(Y)})_{Y\in\pi_{0}(W)})
.
We then
set

w^{*}(C,\rho,\mu)\coloneqq(w^{*}C,w^{*}\rho,w^{*}\mu)\,,

where the right-hand side has the following description.

i.

We set

w^{*}C\coloneqq\bigoplus_{Y\in\pi_{0}(W)}C(w(Y))\,.

ii.

For a subset
B
of
W
and
Y
in
\pi_{0}(W)
we define the projection

(w^{*}\mu)(B)_{Y}\coloneqq\mu_{w(Y)}(w(B\cap Y))

on
C(w(Y))
, see (
10.6
).
We then define
(w^{*}\mu)(B)
as the projection on
w^{*}C

given by

(w^{*}\mu)(B)\coloneqq\oplus_{Y\in\pi_{0}(W)}(w^{*}\mu)(B)_{Y}\,.

By Definition
10.5
.
4
, if
B
is in
{\mathcal{B}}_{W}
, then the sum has finitely many non-zero summands.
One now checks that
w^{*}\mu
is a finitely additive, projection-valued measure which is full. Furthermore,

(w^{*}C,w^{*}\mu)
is locally finite.

iii.

For
g
in
G
, using (
10.5
), we define

\mathclap{(w^{*}\rho)_{g}\colon\bigoplus_{Y\in\pi_{0}(W)}C(w(Y))\xrightarrow{\oplus_{Y}\rho_{g,Y}}\bigoplus_{Y\in\pi_{0}(W)}g(C(w(g^{-1}Y)))\cong g\bigoplus_{Y\in\pi_{0}(W)}C(w(Y))\,.}

The last unitary multiplier isomorphism is fixed by the commutativity of the diagram

\mathclap{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern
